Gaz.</em>, XX, 136.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This is probably the same as the <em>Nitrias</em> of Pliny (VI, 26), the stronghold of the pirates, who threatened the Roman merchants; and may be the <em>Nitra</em> of Ptolemy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">53. <strong>Naura and Tyndis, the first markets of Damirica.</strong> It seems clear that a long stretch of coast on either side of the modern Goa was given a wide berth by foreign merchant-ships because of the piratical habits of its people, and because it produced no cargo of which they were in search.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Like the following ports, Muziris and Nelcynda, these two have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-214" data-page="214" data-folio="204" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n213_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 204">[p. 204]</button> been placed too far north by most of the commentators. The inference from the few words in the Periplus is that the South Konkan and Kanara districts were those more particularly infested by pirates. These may be identified with the Satiya kingdom of Asoka’s inscriptions. The Tamil ports, strictly speaking, lay within the region where the Malayālam language is now spoken, that is, within the modern districts of Malabar, Cochin, and Travancore. The Tulu, Kanarese and Telugu districts seem to be within our author’s <em>Dachinabades</em> rather than his <em>Damirica</em>. These four ports probably lay respectively within the four districts into which the Portuguese and Dutch found the Kerala kingdom divided: Cannanore, Calicut, Cochin and Travancore; of which the last-named, at the time of the Periplus, was held by the Pāndya kingdom.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The four Tamil states, Chola, Pāndya, Kerala, and Satiya, are all named in the 2d Rock Edict of Asoka. (Vincent Smith, <em>Asoka</em>, p. 115). Mr. Smith thinks (<em>Early History</em>, pp. 164, 340-1) that Kerala did not extend north of the Chandragiri river (12° 36′ N.).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Naura being then in North Malabar, may be identified with the modern Cannanore (11° 52′ N., 75° 22′ E.). The latter place is known to have been an active port in the days of the Roman trade, and has yielded one of the most important finds in India of Roman coins, of the reigns of Tiberius, Claudius and Nero.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It seems clear that the identification of this place with the modern Honāvar (14° 17′ N., 74° 27′ E.), while a tempting one, owing to the similarity of names, is not in accord with the facts. Honāvar lies rather within the strip of coast which was in dispute between the Andhra and Saka dynasties, as well as the petty Maurya and Pallava princes; while from similarity of name the modern Cannanore would answer equally well.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The location of Tyndis, of the Chēra kingdom, depends on that of Muziris. It is described as “a village in plain sight on the shore,” and may be identified with the modern Ponnāni (10° 48′ N., 75° 56′ E.). This place lying at the mouth of the river of the same name, which drains a rich section of the western mountains known as the Anaimalai Hills, would have been a natural terminus for the pepper produced there, as well as for the beryls of the Coimbatore district. This Ponnāni river, according to the <em>Imperial Gazetteer</em> (XX, 164), unlike nearly all others on the west coast, is navigable for small vessels for some distance inland.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dr. Burnell prefers Kadalundi near Beypore (11° 11′ N., 75° 49′ E.) on the north bank of the river of the same name, which is also navigable to the foot of the mountains, and carries down large <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-215" data-page="215" data-folio="205" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n214_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 205">[p. 205]</button> quantities of timber. (<em>Imp. Gaz.</em>, VIII, 17.) But the distance of 500 stadia between Tyndis and Muziris indicates Ponnāni.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">53. <strong>Damirica.</strong>—The text has <em>Limyrikê</em>, which previous editions have retained. That name does not appear in India, or in other Roman accounts of it, and it is clearly a corruption caused by the scribe’s confusing the Greek <em>D</em> and <em>L</em>. The name appears in its correct form in the XIIth segment of the Peutinger Tables, almost contemporary with the Periplus, and in Ptolemy as <em>Dimirikê</em>; and there seems no good reason for perpetuating the mistake.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Damirica means the “country of the Tamils,” that is, the Southern Dravidians as they existed in the first century, including particularly the Chēra, Pāndya and Chola kingdoms; known in their own records as <em>Drāvida-dēsam</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">53. <strong>Muziris.</strong>—The location of this port was fixed by Burnell, Caldwell and Yule at <em>Muyiri-kotta</em>, which as Kodungalur or Cranganore (10° 14′ N., 76° 11′ E.), was an important port in mediæval times. Their argument was based on the 7000 stadia named in the text as the distance between Barygaza and Damirica.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Vincent Smith (<em>Early History</em> 340-1) is confident that Muziris and Cranganore are the same. He says “The Kingdom of Satiyaputra must have adjoined Keralaputra; and since the Chandragiri river has always been regarded as the northern boundary of that province, the Satiyaputra Kingdom should probably be identified with that portion of the Konkans—or lowlands between the Western Ghāts and the sea— where the Tulu language is spoken, and of which Mangalore is the center. The name of Kerala is still well remembered and there is no doubt that the Kingdom so called was equivalent to the Southern Konkans or Malabar coast. The ancient capital was Vanji, also named Karuvūr, the <em>Karoura</em> of Ptolemy, situated close to Cranganore; which represents Muziris, the port for the pepper trade, mentioned by Pliny and the author of the Periplus at the end of the first century A. D.” Vanji, according to the <em>Imperial Roll-call</em> (XX, 21), must be placed at the modern Parūr or Paravūr (10° 10′ N., 76° 15′ E.), where the Periyār River empties into the Cochin back-waters. Parūr is still a busy trading center, as well as the headquarters of the district. While now in the district of Travancore, it formerly belonged to Cochin,—that is, to Chēra or Kerala. It is said to comprise almost all the Jews in Travancore; and the settlement may date from the end of the first century, when it is known that there was a considerable Jewish migration to Southern India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The earlier identification of Muziris and Nelcynda placed them at Mangalore and Nīleshwar (12° 52′ N., 74° 51′ E., and 12° 16′ <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-217" data-page="217" data-folio="207" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n216_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 207">[p. 207]</button> N., 75° 8′ E.). This conflicts with nearly all that we know of the geography and politics of the Tamil kingdoms, and is entirely impossible for Nelcynda. This port, according to the Periplus, belonged to the Pāṇḍyan kingdom, which certainly never extended so far north.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-216"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="216" data-folio="206" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n215_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 206">[p. 206]</button></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-016"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-016_p0216.webp" alt="Portion of the Peutinger Tables, from Justus Perthes, Atlas Antiquus." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Portion of the Peutinger Tables, from Justus Perthes, <em>Atlas Antiquus</em>.</figcaption></figure><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-017"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-017_p0217.webp" alt="Map of the Cochin Backwaters showing Cranganore, Cochin, Alleppi, and the Periyar river. — 12 Miles." loading="lazy"><figcaption>12 Miles.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Cochin Backwaters: from Réclus, <em>Asia</em>, Vol. III.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-218"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="218" data-folio="208" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n217_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 208">[p. 208]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The text tells us that Muziris was distant from Tyndis, “by river and sea, 500 stadia,” and Nelcynda from Muziris, “by river and sea, 500 stadia.” This can hardly refer to anything but the Cochin backwaters.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">53. <strong>Nelcynda.</strong>—This port is called <em>the city of the Neacyndi</em>, by Pliny; <em>Melkynda</em> by Ptolemy; <em>Nincylda</em> by the Peutinger Tables, <em>Cyncilim</em> by Friar Odoric, and <em>Nilcinna</em> by the Geographer of Ravenna. It was probably in the backwaters, or thoroughfares, behind Cochin (9° 58′ N., 76° 14′ E.), the exact location being uncertain because of the frequent shifting of river-beds, sand-bars and islands; but certainly very near the modern Kottayam (9° 36′ N., 76° 31′ E.), which is exactly 500 stadia, or 50 miles, from Cranganore. Kottayam, according to the <em>Imperialempty Gazetteer</em> (XVI, 7), is a center of the Syrian Christian community, whose church here is one of the most ancient on the west coast. It is also the natural terminus for the trade-routes from the Pīrmed hills, and is still a trade-center of considerable importance.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The name <em>Nelcynda</em>, Fabricius thinks (p. 160), is the Sanscrit <em>Nīlakantha</em>, “blue neck,” a name of Siva. Caldwell, however, prefers <em>Melkynda</em>, which he translates “Western Kingdom.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A good account of the topography of the coasts of India is given by J. A. Bains (<em>Mill’s International Geography</em>, 1907 ed., p. 469). “The coast-line is singularly devoid of indentations, except at the mouths of the larger rivers and toward the northern portion of the west coast. The only harbors except for light-draft vessels, are found a little way up the deltas of the chief rivers, or where, as at Bombay, a group of islands affords adequate shelter from the open sea. The eastern coast, in particular, is provided with little more than a few imperfectly protected roadsteads. The southern portion of the west coast is distinguished by a series of back-waters, or lagoons, parallel with the coast, and affording a safe and convenient waterway for small vessels when the season of high winds makes the ocean unnavigable.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">54. <strong>Cerobothra.</strong>—This is a transliteration of <em>Chēraputra</em> or <em>Keralaputra</em>, the western Tamil kingdom, which in its greatest extension reached from Cape Comorin to Kārwār Point, nearly 7 degrees of latitude. At the time of the Periplus the northern part had separated, while the southern end had passed to its neighbor, the Pāndyan kingdom; leaving Kerala nearly coterminous with modern Malabar and Cochin districts. The capital was at Karūr, or Parūr, opposite Muziris or Cranganore.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Chēraputra is “son of Chēra,” one of the legendary three brothers who founded the Dravidian power in South India.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-219"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="219" data-folio="209" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n218_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 209">[p. 209]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny’s use of the word as the name of a king was incorrect; it applies to the country, and is also a dynastic name or royal title.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Chēra backwaters seem to be referred to by Pliny in a debated passage on the trade of Ceylon with the “Seres” (VI, 22): “their accounts agreed with the reports of our own merchants, who tell us that the wares which they deposit near those brought for sale by the Seres, on the further bank of a river in their country, are removed by them if they are satisfied with the exchange.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Here <em>Seres</em> must be read as meaning Chēra, the <em>Ch</em> and <em>S</em> being interchanged, just as the neighboring Chola kingdom is always <em>Soli</em> in Sinhalese records.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is quite possible that Chēra is also meant by Pliny’s <em>Seres</em> of XXXIV, 41, who sent the best iron to Rome; this being a product of Haidarābād, and referred to in § 6 of the Periplus, as shipped from India to Adulis. See also under Sarapis, p. 146.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The “silent trade,” noted by Fa-Hien in Ceylon itself, is referred to under § 65, and again by Pliny (VI, 20), Pausanias (III, xii, 3), and Cosmas Indicopleustes (book II).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For further references to Chēra and the other Tamil states growing out of the original establishment at Korkai, see Vincent Smith, <em>Early History</em>, Chap. xvi;—Caldwell, <em>Grammar of the Dravidian Languages</em>, introduction; also <em>History of Tinnevelly</em>;—Burnell, <em>South Indian Palæography</em>;—Shanguni Menon, <em>History of Travancore</em>;—Francis Day, <em>The Land of the Permauls</em>;—J. B. Pandian, <em>Indian Village Folk</em>;—Sir Walter Elliot, <em>Coins of Southern India</em>;—Foulkes, <em>The Civilization of the Dakhan down to the 6th century B. C.</em>, in <em>Indian Antiquary</em>, 1879, pp. 1-10;— K. P. Padmanabha Menon, <em>Notes on Malabar and its place-names</em>, in <em>Indian Antiquary</em>, Aug., 1902;—Wilson, <em>The Pāndyas</em>, in <em>Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society</em>, iii, 199;—Dawson, <em>The Chēras</em>, in J. R. A. S., viii, 1;—Sewell, <em>Lists of Inscriptions, and Sketch of the Dynasties of Southern India</em>, in the <em>Archæological Survey</em>, Madras, 1884;—F. Kielhorn, <em>Dates of Chola and Pāndya Kings</em>, in <em>Epigraphia Indica</em>, Vols. IV-VIII, inclusive;—<em>Imperial Gazetteer</em>, Vol. II, Chaps. i, iii, iv, v, ix;—Bühler, <em>Indische Palæographie</em>, and generally, his <em>Grundriss der Indo-Arischen Philologie und Altertumskunde</em>;—Fleet, <em>The Dynasties of the Kanarese Districts</em>, and Bhandarkar, <em>Early History of the Dekkan</em>, in <em>Gazetteer of the Bombay Presidency</em>, I, ii;—Loventhal, <em>Coins of Tinnevelly</em>; —Hultzsch, <em>South Indian Inscriptions</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">54. <strong>Abounds in ships.</strong>—In these protected thoroughfares flourished a sea-trade, largely in native Dravidian craft, which was of early creation and of great influence in the interchange of ideas as well as commodities, not only in South India, but in the Persian Gulf, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-220" data-page="220" data-folio="210" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n219_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 210">[p. 210]</button> and the coasts of Arabia and Africa, with which the trade was principally maintained. Both Buddhist and Brahman writings testify to its existence in the 5th century B. C.; but their evidence is late, as they are the product of the Northern Aryans, an inland race, who appeared in South India after its activities had been widely developed. Better evidence is given by the Dravidian alphabet, supposed to be from a Semitic (Himyaritic, or Phœnician) original, and to date from about 1000 B. C., whereas the Aryan, or Kharosthī, alphabet was formulated after the conquest, about 500 B. C. (R. Sewell, <em>Hindu Period of Southern India</em>, in <em>Imp. Gaz.</em>, II, 322.)</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-018"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-018_p0220.webp" alt="Merchant-ship of the 2d century, from a relief on a sarcophagus in the Lateran Museum." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Merchant-ship of the 2d century, from a relief on a sarcophagus in the Lateran Museum.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Sent from Arabia and by the Greeks” were the ships found by our author in the Chēra backwaters. The text has <em>Ariaca</em>, but the error is obvious, as the articles of trade were from foreign, and not Hindu, sources. “No Aryan language had penetrated into these kingdoms, which lived their own life, completely secluded from Northern India, and in touch with the outer world only through the medium of maritime commerce, which had been conducted with safety from very early times. The pearls of the Gulf of Manār, the beryls of Coimbatore, and the pepper of Malabar were not to be had elsewhere, and were largely sought by foreign merchants, as early as the 7th or 8th century B. C.” (Vincent Smith, <em>Early History</em>, 334.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-221"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="221" data-folio="211" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n220_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 211">[p. 211]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Benjamin of Tudela, in the 12th century, gives the following account of trade on this coast:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Thence is seven days’ journey to Khulam (Quilon) which is the beginning of the country of the Sun-worshippers. These are the sons of Cush, who read the stars and are all black in color. They are honest in commerce. When merchants come to them from distant lands and enter the harbour, three of the King’s secretaries go down to them and record their names and then bring them before the King, whereupon the King makes himself responsible even for their property which they leave in the open unprotected. There is an official who sits in his office, and the owner of any lost property has only to describe it to him when he hands it back. This custom prevails in all that country. From Passover to New Year, that is all during the summer, no man can go out of his house because of the sun, for the heat in that country is intense, and from the third hour of the day onward, everybody remains in his house until evening. Then they go forth and kindle lights in all the market places and all the streets, and then do their work and business at night-time. For they have to turn night into day in consequence of the great heat of the sun. Pepper is found there. They plant the trees thereof in the fields, and each man of the city knows his own plantation. The trees are small and the pepper is as white as snow. And when they have collected it they place it in sauce-pans and pour boiling water over it, so that it may become strong. Then they take it out of the water and dry it in the sun, and it turns black. Cinnamon and ginger and many other kinds of spices are found in this land.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">54. <strong>Pandian kingdom.</strong>—This was Pāndya, the southernmost, and traditionally the earliest, of the three Tamil states. Roughly it coincided with the modern districts of Tinnevelly and Madurā; at the time of the Periplus it extended beyound the Ghāts and included Travancore. The capital, originally at Korkai (the <em>Colchi</em> of § 59, which see) had been removed to Madurā (9° 55′ N., 78° 7′ E.).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Here too, as in the Chēra kingdom, the name is used for the country and as a dynastic title, not as the name of any king.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">55. <strong>Bacarē.</strong>—(Ptolemy gives <em>Barkarē</em>, which is perhaps the preferable reading.) This place, distant 120 stadia from Nelcynda, at an inlet of the sea, can be no other than Porakād (9° 22′ N., 76° 22′ E.), for which it is a close transliteration; while the distance from Kottayam is exactly in accord with the text.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Porakād was once a notable port, but declined with the rise of Alleppey, built a few miles farther north after a canal had been cut <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-222" data-page="222" data-folio="212" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n221_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 212">[p. 212]</button> through from sea to backwater and harbor works constructed. (<em>Imp. Gaz.</em>, XX, 188.) The Portuguese, and subsequently the Dutch, had settlements at Porakād. It is mentioned by Varthema (1503) as <em>Porcai</em>, and by Tavernier (1648) as <em>Porca</em>. The remains of a Portuguese fort and factory are now covered by the sea, being visible at low water. (Ball, in his edition of Tavernier, I, 241.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Here also is the mouth of the Achenkoil river, which rises in the Ghāts near the Shencottah pass, the main highway between Travancore and Tinnevelly.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">According to Menon (<em>Notes on Malabar and its place-names</em>), the settlements were nearly all east of the backwaters at the Christian era, and the present beaches existed only as tide-shoals. During the middle ages there was a period of elevation, which led to the formation of new islands, while floods from the mountains changed the courses of the rivers, and the location of the inlets. At present the tendency is toward subsidence, houses built at Cochin a century ago being now under water. About 800 B. C., according to local tradition, the sea reached the hills.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Megasthenes, in the 4th century B. C., mentioned as “‘on the sea-coast’’ the town of Tropina (Tripontari) now on the mainland side of the backwaters; Ptolemy’s three shore towns between Muziris and <em>Barkarē</em> are likewise on the land side.</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-019"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-019_p0222.webp" alt="Outrigger canoe or coastal boat under sail with occupants on water." loading="lazy"></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Large ships.</strong>—The increase in the size of shipping following the discovery of Hippalus is referred to also in § 10. Pliny speaks <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-223" data-page="223" data-folio="213" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n222_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 213">[p. 213]</button> of the same thing in describing the trade between Malabar and Ceylon. “The navigation,” he says (VI, 24), “was formerly confined to vessels made of rushes, rigged in the manner familiar on the Nile. The vessels of recent times are built with prows at either end so that there may be no need of turning around while sailing in these channels, which are extremely narrow. The tonnage of the vessels is 3,000 amphoræ.” (About 33 tons.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By “double prows” Pliny probably means some such build and rig as shown in the accompanying illustration, which is typical of the Indian Ocean generally. Mast and sail can be reversed at will, so that the craft can be sailed in either direction.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Pepper,</strong> black and white.—<em>Piper nigrum</em>, Linn., order <em>Piperaceæ</em>. A perennial climber, wild in the forests of Travancore and Malabar, and extensively cultivated from very early times, in the hot, damp localities of Southern India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lassen (I, 278), notes that the Greek word <em>peperi</em>, Latin <em>piper</em>, simply repeats the Indian name <em>pippali</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The antiquity of the trade in pepper is not so easily shown as that in other spices. There is no certain mention of it in the Egyptian inscriptions. In the Hebrew scriptures it is unknown, nor has it a place among the “mint and anise and cummin” of the Gospels. Herodotus has no bit of folklore to attach to it. Theophrastus, indeed, in the 4th century B. C., knows it as a medicine, and Dioscorides distinguishes between black, white and long pepper. The Sanscrit writers describe it as a medicine for fever and dyspepsia, used together with ginger and long pepper; these were their “three pungent substances.” (<em>Mahāvagga</em>, VI, 19, 1; see also I-tsing, <em>Record of Buddhist Practices</em> [7th century A. D.], chap. xxviii; Takakusu’s edition, p. 135.) The Romans had it after their conquests in Asia Minor, Syria and Egypt, and at once provided the greatest market for it. Egypt knew it, probably, through the sea-trade of the Ptolemies; Syria through the caravan-trade to Tyre from the Persian Gulf. There is some reason for supposing that pepper was the spice more especially in demand in Babylonia and the Persian Gulf trade generally, just as cinnamon was that more especially reserved for Egypt; and that the most active demand for it came with the extension of the Persian empire under Darius. The trade was by sea and not overland; Herodotus knows the Dravidians (III, 100) only as having “a complexion closely resembling the Aethiopians,” and as being “situated very far from the Persians, toward the south, and never subject to Darius.” It may also be surmised that a steady demand for pepper existed in China before it arose in Rome, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-224" data-page="224" data-folio="214" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n223_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 214">[p. 214]</button> that this was one reason for the sailing of the junks to the Malabar coast in the 2d century B. C. and probably earlier. In Marco Polo’s day the tonnage of the junks was calculated according to their capacity in baskets of pepper; and he found (II, lxxxii) “for one shipload of pepper that goes to Alexandria or elsewhere, destined for Christendom, there come a hundred such, aye and more too, to this haven of Zayton” (Chwan-chau, above Amoy).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The trade in pepper in the time of the Roman Empire brought the merchants unheard-of profits just as it did later the Genoese and Venetians. It was one of the most important articles of commerce between India and Rome, supplying perhaps three-quarters of the total bulk of the average westbound cargo.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The constant use of pepper in the most expensive Roman cookery is reflected by its price, quoted by Pliny (XII, 14) as 15 denarii, or about $2.55 per lb.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Among the offerings by the emperor Constantine to the church under St. Silvester, were costly vessels and fragrant gums and spices, including frankincense, nard, balsam, storax, myrrh, cinnamon, saffron and pepper.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">That it continued in high esteem is shown by the terms offered by Alaric for raising the siege of Rome: “the immediate payment of 5,000 lbs. of gold, of 30,000 lbs. of silver, of 4,000 robes of silk, of 3,000 pcs. of fine scarlet cloth, and of 3,000 lbs. weight of pepper.” (Gibbon, <em>Decline and Fall</em>, III, 271-2.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny, indeed, expresses surprise at the taste that brought it into so great favor (XII, 14): “It is quite surprising that the use of pepper has come so much into fashion, seeing that in other substances which we use, it is sometimes their sweetness, and sometimes their appearance that has attracted our notice; whereas, pepper has nothing in it that can plead as a recommendation to either fruit or berry, its only desirable quality being a certain pungency; and yet it is for this that we import it all the way from India! Who was the first to make trial of it as an article of food? And who, I wonder, was the man that was not content to prepare himself by hunger only for the satisfying of a greedy appetite?”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In mediæval Europe the trade was highly organized, the spice being handled especially by merchants called “pepperers;” and the prices quoted in Rogers’ <em>History of Agriculture and Prices in England</em> show that in the years just prior to the Portuguese discovery of the Cape route, a pound of pepper brought two shillings, being four days’ pay for a carpenter! Yet the people preferred it above all other <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-225" data-page="225" data-folio="215" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n224_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 215">[p. 215]</button> spices; it was the first thing asked for by ‘‘Glutton’’ in <em>Piers Plowman</em> (V, 310-13):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza"><span class="line">‘‘I haue gode ale, gossib,’’ quod she˙ ‘‘glotown, wiltow assaye?’’</span><br>
<span class="line">‘‘Hastow aughte in thi purs˙ any hote spices?’’</span><br>
<span class="line">‘‘I haue peper and piones,’’ quod she˙ ‘‘and a pounde of garlike,</span><br>
<span class="line">A ferthyngworth of fenel-seed˙ for fastyngdayes.’’</span></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Friar Odoric (Chap. iii) describes the pepper production of ‘‘Minibar’’ as follows: ‘‘the wood in which it grows containeth in circuit eighteen days’ journey. And in the said wood or forest there are two cities, one called Flandrina, and the other Cyncilim’’ (probably Nelcynda). ‘‘In the aforesaid wood pepper is had after this manner: first it groweth in leaves like unto pot-herbs, which they plant near unto great trees as we do our vines, and they bring forth pepper in clusters, as our vines do yield grapes, but being ripe, they are of a green color, and are gathered as we gather grapes, and then the grains are laid in the sun to be dried, and being dried are put into earthen vessels; and thus is pepper made and kept. . . At the south end of the said forests stands the city of Polumbrum, which aboundeth with merchandise of all kinds.’’ (The proper form would be Polumbum, the Latinized version of Polum or Kolum, the modern Quilon. <em>P</em> and <em>K</em> are interchanged here as in the case of Karūr, the modern Parūr.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Tavernier found pepper sold principally at Tuticorin and Calicut. Some, however, came from Rājāpur on the Ratnāgiri coast. ‘‘The Dutch,’’ he says (II, xii. Ball’s ed.), ‘‘who purchase it from the <em>Malabaris</em> do not pay in cash for it, but exchange for it many kinds of merchandise, as cotton, opium, vermilion, and quicksilver, and it is this pepper which is exported to Europe. . . . 500 <em>livres</em> of it brings only 38 <em>reals</em>, but on the merchandise which they give in exchange they gain 100 per cent. One can get it for the equivalent in money of 28 or 30 <em>reals</em> cash, but to purchase it in that way would be much more costly than the Dutch method.’’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He mentions also (I, xvi) a large storehouse kept by the Portuguese at Cochin, called the ‘‘Pepper House.’’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">See also Watt, 896-901;—Flückiger and Hanbury, <em>Pharmacographia</em>, p. 579;—<em>Encyclopædia Britannica</em>, article ‘‘Pepper;’’—Brandis, <em>Indian Trees</em>;—Vignoli, <em>Liber Pontificalis</em>, Rome, 1724-55.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Odoric also describes a propitiation of the serpents guarding the pepper, similar to those of the frankincense and diamond; the story is better in the version of ‘‘Sir John Mandeville’’ (Chap. xviii): ‘‘In that country be many manner of serpents and of other vermin for the great heat of the country and of the pepper. And some men say, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-226" data-page="226" data-folio="216" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n225_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 216">[p. 216]</button> that when they will gather the pepper, they make fire, to burn about to make the serpents and the cockodrills to flee. But save their grace of all that say so. For if they burnt about the trees that bear, the pepper should be burnt, and it would dry up all the virtue, as of any other thing; and then they did themselves much harm, and they should never quench the fire. But thus they do: they anoint their hands and their feet with a juice made of snails and of other things made therefor, of the which the serpents and the venomous beasts hate and dread the savour; and that maketh them flee before them, because of the smell, and then they gather it surely enough.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This belief in the guarding of treasure, or of wealth-producing trees, or the habitation thereof, by spirits in the form of serpents, has already been noted as attaching to frankincense (§ 29), and will appear likewise with the diamond (§ 56). The supposed necessity of appeasing or else expelling the serpents by the use of other substances was held strongly in Rome itself. Pliny ascribes this power to galbanum, “a kind of giant fennel” (XII, 56). “If ignited in a pure state it has the property of driving away serpents by its smoke.” And again (XXIV, 13), “the very touch of it, mingled with oil and spondylium, is sufficient to kill a serpent.” So also Virgil (<em>Georgics</em>, III, 415):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza"><span class="line">“Galbaneoque agitare graves nidore chelydros.”</span></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The frankincense gatherers depended on burning storax; see under § 29, pp. 131-2.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Malabathrum.</strong>—Heeren, Vincent and McCrindle translate this “betel,” and thereby accuse the Periplus of a blunder in §§ 63 and 65, where the substance is described as coming from the Himalaya mountains. The translation rests on an assumption that the <em>petros</em> of the text in § 65 is the same as the Portuguese <em>betre</em> or <em>betle</em> meaning betel.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Watt (p. 891) says this latter is rather derived from a Malay word <em>vettila</em> or <em>vern-ila</em>, meaning “leaf,” and it is very doubtful if the betel of modern times entered into international commerce in the Roman period.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The word <em>petros</em> is rather from the Sanscrit <em>patra</em>, “leaf,” of the <em>tamala</em> tree which, as explained under §§ 10, 13 and 14, is a variety of cinnamon or laurel. The leaf exported from Southern India was also from <em>Cinnamomum iners</em>, and possibly from the <em>Cinnamomum zeylanicum</em> which in later times was cultivated in Ceylon and is one of the sources of our cinnamon. (See Tavernier, <em>Travels</em>, II, xii). The leaf coming from the Himalaya mountains was principally from the <em>Cinnamomum tamala</em>, which was native there. Pliny <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-227" data-page="227" data-folio="217" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n226_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 217">[p. 217]</button> says that the <em>malabathrum</em> which entered so prominently into Roman perfumes should have a smell like nard, and other Roman writers seem to have confused it with the Ganges nard mentioned in § 63. (See also Lassen, I, 279-285; II, 555-561.) Horace, (II, vii, 89), refers to it as follows:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza"><span class="line">————“Coronatus nitentes</span><br>
<span class="line">Malobathro Syrio capillos.”</span></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Malabathrum and spikenard were the two most treasured ingredients of the ointments and perfumes of the Roman empire.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A curious trade condition is suggested by the fact that the Romans knew cinnamon and cassia only as coming from the Somali coast of Africa, while they knew the malabathrum as coming from various parts of India; and yet the malabathrum was, in at least one case, the leaf from the same tree that produced a variety of cinnamon. The Periplus in no place mentions the export of cinnamon from India, but in §§ 56 and 63 describes the export of <em>malabathrum</em>. This seems to indicate a trade monopoly of very ancient date and thorough enforcement, by which the bark only went for trade purposes to the African coast, while the leaf was an open article of trade to India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lindsay (<em>History of Merchant Shipping and Ancient Commerce</em>, I, 156-7), also remarks on this “striking instance of the secrecy with which the ancients conducted the more valuable portions of their trade.” Herodotus, he thinks, “could only have obtained his information about cinnamon from the merchants who traded along the shores of Malabar . . . who kept the secret of its <em>provenance</em> as the Carthaginians kept that of British tin.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Another letter from Mr. R. E. Drake-Brockman, dated Berbera, April 27, 1910, gives further confirmation of the absence of the cinnamon species from the Somali peninsula. (See under § 13, p. 87).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“It is unlikely that the original inhabitants of this country knew anything of cinnamon until they had heard of its commercial value from the natives of India or Arabs, who have been known to the coastal people from the earliest times. These same traders, if they penetrated into the interior at all, which is extremely doubtful, would have hunted for anything of any commercial value, and if cinnamon had existed they would have continued to export it up to the present day as they do frankincense, myrrh and gum arabic. A point which is worthy of notice is that the Somalis have names for all the last three, whereas they have had to go to the Arabic language for their names for cinnamon. They know of two varieties, <em>koronfol</em> and <em>karfa</em>, both of which are imported.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“It is highly probable that both Strabo and Pliny were led to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-228" data-page="228" data-folio="218" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n227_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 218">[p. 218]</button> believe that the myrrh, frankincense, cinnamon and spices pouring into the kingdom of Aethiopia and upper Egypt all came from the same place. Possibly traders in Aethiopia obtained a better price for their myrrh and cinnamon if they stated the difficulties and dangers they experienced collecting it in the countries of the savage Gallas or their antecedents in the Horn of Africa.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>‘‘There can be no doubt that the natives of these regions have always been greatly feared by their less warlike neighbors. The Somalis and their antecedents have always been keen traders, and there can be little doubt that if cinnamon ever existed in these regions, the practice of collecting it would not have been dropped unless the species here collected was of a very inferior quality and gradually lost its marketable value.’’</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Through the courtesy of the same gentleman in gathering specimens of the various aromatic gums of Somaliland, a more positive statement may be made than was possible under § 32, pp. 141-2, concerning the Egyptian frankincense trade, in determining the character of the trees depicted on the Punt reliefs at Deir el Bahri, a photograph of which was reproduced on page 120.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Professor Breasted in his <em>Ancient Records of Egypt</em> (II, 263-5), calls this tree <em>myrrh</em>, and translates it as myrrh wherever the records refer to it. In the publications of the Egypt Exploration Fund (<em>The Temple of Deir-el-Bahri</em>, III, 12), it is called <em>frankincense</em>, but is located in Somaliland in the neighborhood of Mosyllum, because of the supposed African appearance of the Punt people who appear elsewhere in the reliefs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Specimens of true myrrh sent from Somaliland show clearly that no sculptor could have intended to depict by the rich foliage on the reliefs, the bare, thorny, trifoliate but almost leafless myrrh tree, nor yet the almost equally leafless varieties of Somaliland frankincense. This tree is clearly <em>Boswellia Carteri</em>, the frankincense of the rich plain of Dhofar in Southern Arabia. This is the only place producing frankincense where the trees can be cultivated on a fertile plain by the shore, in the midst of green fields and cattle. There is no place on the African coast which meets these conditions. Naville’s objection that the natives are ‘‘not Arabs,’’ <em>i. e.</em>, not Semitic, is really in favor of such a belief; they were the pre-Semitic, Cushite race whose dominions centered at Dhofar, and who are represented there by the modern Gara tribe. There can be no question that the trees in that relief are the frankincense of Dhofar, the ‘‘Sachalitic frankincense’’ of the Periplus, the modern <em>Shehri luban</em>.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-229"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="229" data-folio="219" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n228_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 219">[p. 219]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To the possible objection that the Darror and Nogal valleys, in the southern part of the Somali peninsula, are fertile and might produce a better foliage than the northern coast, it may be said that the fertility stops far short of the east coast, which is absolutely desert; whereas the reliefs show a rich and fertile plain bordering the sea.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>A great quantity of coin.</strong>—The drain of specie from Rome to the East has already been referred to under § 49, and is bitterly condemned by Pliny. “The subject,” he says (VI, 26), “is one well worthy of our notice, seeing that in no year does India drain us of less than 550,000,000 <em>sesterces</em> ($22,000,000) giving back her own wares, which are sold among us at fully 100 times their first cost.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A generation before the Periplus, in 22 A. D., this was made the subject of a letter from the emperor Tiberius to the Roman Senate:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“If a reform is in truth intended, where must it begin? and how am I to restore the simplicity of ancient times? . . . How shall we reform the taste for dress? . . . How are we to deal with the peculiar articles of feminine vanity, and in particular with that rage for jewels and precious trinkets, which drains the empire of its wealth, and sends, in exchange for baubles, the money of the Commonwealth to foreign nations, and even to the enemies of Rome?” (Tacitus, <em>Annals</em>, iii, 53.)</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This extravagant importation of luxuries from the East without adequate production of commodities to offer in exchange, was the main cause of the successive depreciation and degradation of the Roman currency, leading finally to its total repudiation. The monetary standard of Rome was established by accumulations of precious metal resulting from its wars. The sack of the rich city of Tarentum in 272 B. C., enabled Rome to change her coinage from copper to silver. After the destruction of Carthage and Corinth in 146 B. C., gold coinage came into general use, and through the wars of Cæsar gold became so plentiful that in 47 B. C. its ratio to silver was as 1 to 8.9, lower than ever before or since. Under Augustus the ratio was about 1 to 9.3, the <em>aureus</em> being worth 25 silver <em>denarii</em>. Under Claudius the sea-route to India was opened, after which came the reign of Nero, marked by every form of wastefulness and extravagance, during which the silver <em>denarius</em> fell from 1-84 to 1-96 pound of silver, an alloy of 20 per cent copper being added to it. Under Trajan the alloy reached 30 per cent, and under Septimius Severus 50 per cent. Finally, under Elagabalus, 218 A. D., the <em>denarius</em> had become wholly copper and was repudiated. Even the golden <em>aureus</em> was tampered with. Exported in large quantities to become the basis of exchange in India, the supply at home was exhausted. Under <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-230" data-page="230" data-folio="220" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n229_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 220">[p. 220]</button> Augustus the <em>aureus</em> weighed 1-40 of a pound of gold, and under Diocletian it weighed but 1-60. Under Constantine it fell to 1-72, when the coin was taken only by weight (Sabatier, <em>Monnaies Byzan- tines</em>, i, 51-2; Brooks Adams, <em>Law of Civilization and Decay</em>, 25-8). It was this steady loss of capital, to replace which no new wealth was produced, that led finally to the abandonment of Rome and to the transfer of the capital at the end of the 3d century to Nicomedia and soon afterward to Byzantium.</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-020"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-020_p0230.webp" alt="Coin of Nero commemorating the opening of the harbor-works at Ostia." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Coin of Nero commemorating the opening of the harbor-works at Ostia.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the Madras Government Museum there is nearly a complete series of the coins of the Roman Emperors during the period of active trade with India, all of them excavated in southern India. A notable fact is that there are two distinct breaks in the series; which may of course be supplied by later discovery, but which seem to indicate a cessation of trade due to political turmoil in Rome. The coins of Tiberius, Caligula, Claudius and Nero are numerous. There are very few of Vespasian and Titus anywhere in India. Those of Domitian, Nerva, Trajan and Hadrian are frequent; then there comes another break lasting until the time of Commodus. This indication, so far as it has any value, points again to the dating of the Periplus during the reign of Nero rather than during those of Vespasian and Titus.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For a full account of Roman coins discovered in South India, see E. Thurston, Catalogue No. 2, Madras Government Museum, pp. 1-47.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Crude glass.</strong>—The origin of the glass industry in India is uncertain. According to Mitra, <em>Antiquities of Orissa</em>, I, 101, it was made in Ceylon in the 3d century B. C., and Pliny (XXXVI, 66) refers to the glass of India as superior to all others, because “made of pounded crystal.” Mirrors, with a foil of lead and tin, were largely used there at the time of the Periplus, and Pliny indicates (XXXVII, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-231" data-page="231" data-folio="221" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n230_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 221">[p. 221]</button> 20) that “the people of India, by coloring crystal, have found a method of imitating various precious stones, beryls in particular.” An early play, the <em>Mrichchhakatikā</em> or <em>Little Clay Cart</em>, gives a scene in a court of justice to this effect (Mitra, <em>op. cit.</em>, 100; see also A. W. Ryder’s translation, Cambridge, 1905):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Do you know these ornaments?”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Have I not said? They may be different, though like; I cannot say more; they may be imitations by some skillful artist.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“It is true; provost, examine them; they may be different, though like; the dexterity of the artists is no doubt very great, and they readily fabricate imitations of ornaments they have once seen, in such a manner that the difference shall scarcely be discernible.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Copper, tin, and lead.</strong>—As at Barygaza, intended chiefly for the coinage. So Pliny (XXXIV, 17): “India has neither brass nor lead, but exchanges precious stones and pearls for them.” The Indian coins were of lead, slightly alloyed with either copper or tin. (Sir Walter Elliot, <em>Coins of Southern India</em>, p. 22.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lead was used also, mixed with a little tin in thin sheets, as a foil for the manufacture of mirrors. (Mitra, <em>op. cit.</em>, p. 101.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Orpiment.</strong>—This is the yellow sulphide of arsenic, appearing in the form of smooth shining scales, which have long been an article of export from the Persian Gulf to India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny (VI, 26) says, “Next to these is the nation of the Ori and then the Hyctanis (Rud Shur?) a river of Carmania, with an excellent harbor at its mouth, and producing gold; at this spot the writers state that for the first time they caught sight of the Great Bear. The star Arcturus too, they tell us, was not to be seen here every night, and never when it was seen, during the whole of it. Up to this spot extended the empire of the Achæmenidæ, and in these districts are to be found mines of copper, iron, arsenic, and red lead.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The principal use of orpiment was as a yellow pigment—<em>auri pigmentum</em>—making a durable mineral paint, as did realgar and lapis lazuli.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Wheat for the sailors.</strong>—Marco Polo also notes (III, xvii), “No wheat grows in this province, but rice only.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Cottonara.</strong>—Dr. Burnell derives this from <em>Kolatta-nādū</em>, which he identifies with North Malabar, of which Cannanore and Tellicherry are the centers. Dr. Buchanan prefers <em>Kadatta-nādū</em>, South Malabar, on either side of Calicut. In mediæval times the domain of the Rājās of <em>Kolatnād</em> included both. Bishop Caldwell, in his <em>Dravidian Grammar</em>, derives the name from Malayālam <em>kadatta</em>, transport or conveyance, and <em>nādū</em>, district. Menon (<em>Indian Antiquary</em>, Aug. 1902), suggests <em>kadal</em>, sea, or <em>kōdu</em>, mountain; and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-232" data-page="232" data-folio="222" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n231_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 222">[p. 222]</button> <em>kōdu-nādū</em>, the hill-country back of the sea-coast, would accord with the facts while supporting the transliteration of the text. In any case the term does not seem to have been applied to an exact locality.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Great quantities of fine pearls.</strong>—These were from the fisheries of the Gulf of Manār, mentioned in § 59, and brought to be sold in the Chēra ports, the meeting-point of Eastern and Western trade.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Silk cloth.</strong>—From China, by way of Tibet and the Ganges. See under §§ 39, 49 and 64.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Gangetic spikenard.</strong>—See under § 63.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Transparent stones.</strong>—These were principally the beryls of the Coimbatore district, for which there was a constant demand in Rome, and which always found their principal foreign market in the Malabar ports. This localization of the gem trade continued until after the Portuguese period in India; the reason is stated by Tavernier (II, xxi):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Goa was formerly the place where there was the largest trade in all Asia in diamonds, rubies, sapphires, topazes, and other stones. All the miners and merchants went there to sell the best which they had obtained at the mines, because they had there full liberty to sell, whereas, in their own country, if they showed anything to the kings and princes, they were compelled to sell at whatever price they pleased to fix. There was also at Goa a large trade in pearls, both of those which came from the island of Bahrein in the Persian Gulf, and those fished for in the Straits of Manār on the coast of the island of Ceylon.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">India and Ceylon were preeminently the source of production of precious stones of all kinds, which were exported to every part of the civilized world. Watt (p. 556) classifies the production as follows:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>1. The Beryl group, from the sea-green aquamarine to the white. (The <em>beryllium</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 20.)</li><li>2. Diamond. (The <em>adamas</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 15.)</li><li>3. Pearl.</li><li>4. Ruby. (The <em>carbunculus</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 25.)</li><li>5. Sapphire, occurring in numerous colors, various blues, violet, yellow, green and white. Produced mainly on the Southern Malabar hills, now rarely found in India but more frequently in Ceylon. (The <em>hyacinthus</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 41.)</li><li>6. Spinel. (Included among the 12 varieties of Pliny’s <em>carbunculus</em>.)</li><li>7. Topaz. Watt doubts its production in India at any place, and the Periplus shows on the contrary that it was imported</li></ul></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-233"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="233" data-folio="223" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n232_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 223">[p. 223]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>from the Red Sea. (The <em>chrysolithos</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 42.)</li><li>8. Turquoise. A product of Persia, not occurring in India but reaching the northwestern ports of trade. (The <em>callaina</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 33.)</li><li>9. Garnet. Common in many parts of India; those of Rajputana being the best. (One of the 12 varieties, perhaps the <em>alabandic</em>, of Pliny’s <em>carbunculus</em>.)</li><li>10. Jade and Jadeite; found mainly in Turkestan but also in upper Burma, while a serpentine from Afghanistan is often substituted. While not produced in India, these all find their way to Indian markets. The leading market is China.</li><li>11. Lapis Lazuli, or ultramarine; also from Persia. Largely used for decoration of all kinds and in demand in India, Egypt and the Mediterranean world from the earliest times. (The <em>sapphiros</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 39.)</li><li>12. Quartzose, including</li><li>a. Rock crystals, white and colored, which the Romans do not seem to have distinguished from more precious stones. (The <em>crystal</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 9-10.)</li><li>b. Agate, carnelian, bloodstone, chrysoprase, jasper, chalcedony, cat’s eye, onyx, opal, etc. (<em>Achates, murrhine; sarda; heliotropium; chrysoprasus; iaspis, carchedonia; sardonyx; astrobolos; onyx; opal</em> (Pliny, XXXVII.)</li><li>13. Tourmalines, varying from black through red, dark blue, olive green, and white, the red varieties being commonest in India. (The <em>lychnis</em> of Pliny, XXXVII, 29.)</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For further discussion of the deposits and trade, see Lassen, I, 229-43; Tavernier, II.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Beryls,” says Pliny (XXXVII, 20), “are produced in India, and are rarely to be found elsewhere. The lapidaries cut all beryls of a hexagonal form, because the color, which is deadened by a dull uniformity of the surface, is heightened by the reflection from the angles. If they are cut in any other way, these stones have no brilliancy whatever.” (The crystals are naturally hexahedral.) “The most esteemed beryls are those which in color resemble the pure green of the sea. . . . The people of India are marvelously fond of beryls of an elongated form, and say that these are the only precious stones they prefer wearing without the addition of gold.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the <em>Mrichchhakatikā</em>, an early Sanscrit play, there is a scene which includes a row of jewelers’ shops, “where skillful artists are examining pearls, topazes, sapphires, beryls, rubies, lapis lazuli, coral <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-234" data-page="234" data-folio="224" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n233_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 224">[p. 224]</button> and other jewels; some set rubies in gold; some work with gold ornaments on colored thread, some string pearls, some grind the lapis lazuli, some pierce shells, and some cut coral.” (Mitra, <em>op. cit.</em>, p. 100.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Diamonds.</strong>—The text is <em>adamas</em>. Some commentators, notably Dana, have doubted whether the Romans ever knew the true diamond. There can be no doubt that Pliny in his description (XXXVII, 15) includes under <em>adamas</em> other substances, probably quartz, iron ore, emery, etc., but he also says that the diamond possessed the greatest value, not only among the precious stones, but of all human possessions; and as Watt says (p. 556), India was long the only source of diamonds known to European nations.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Garcia de Orta (1563), mentions various Eastern diamond mines, such as those of “Bisnager” (Vijayanagar) and the “Decam” (Deccan). Ball, in his translation of Tavernier’s <em>Travels</em>, gives full particulars of all the Indian sources of diamonds (II, 450-461). Tavernier was a diamond merchant and the first European (1676) to examine critically the diamonds and court jewels of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The principal districts were,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>(1) Southern Group:—districts of Kadapa, Bellary, Karnul, Kistnā, Godāverī, (Golconda, etc.);</li><li>(2) Middle Group:—Mahānadī valley, districts of Sambalpur, Chanda;</li><li>(3) Northern Group:—Vindhyan conglomerates near Panna (still worked).</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny (XXXVII, 15) describes the Indian <em>adamas</em> as “found, not in a stratum of gold, but in a substance of a kindred nature to crystal; which it closely resembles in its transparency and its highly polished hexangular and hexahedral forms.” (The true form of the diamond is octahedral.) “In shape it is turbinated, running to a point at either extremity, and closely resembling, marvelous to think of, two cones united at the base. In size, too, it is as large even as a hazel-nut.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Romans seem to have had no knowledge of diamondcutting. Pliny goes on to say that “its hardness is beyond all expression, while at the same time it quite sets fire at defiance; owing to which indomitable powers it has received the name which it derives from the Greek.” (<em>a</em> privative, and <em>damaō</em>, “to subdue.”)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After his description of the hardness of the diamond, Pliny observes, “this indomitable power, which sets at naught the two most violent agents in nature, fire, namely, and iron, is made to yield before <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-235" data-page="235" data-folio="225" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n234_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 225">[p. 225]</button> the blood of a he-goat. The blood, however, must be fresh and warm; the stone, too, must be well steeped in it.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Ball (Tavernier, <em>Travels</em>, II, 460-1), quotes a story from Nicol Conti (15th century) about Indian diamonds obtainable only by flinging pieces of meat on the mountain,’’ where the diamonds could not be collected owing to the number of serpents. The pieces of meat with diamonds sticking to them were then carried to their nests by birds of prey, from whence they were recovered by diamond seekers. . . . This myth is founded on the very common practice in India on the opening of a mine, to offer up cattle to propitiate the evil spirits who are supposed to guard treasures—these being represented by the myth. At such sacrifices birds of prey assemble to pick up what they can;’’ which is the foundation for the remainder of the story.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Here we have a striking similarity to the beliefs connected with the gathering of frankincense, as outlined under § 29, and pepper (§ 56).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Thousand Nights and One Night</em> gives substantially the same story (dxliv-v; Sinbad the Sailor, 2d voyage), while sufficiently identifying the stone:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Walking along the valley I found that its soil was of diamond, the stone wherewith they pierce jewels and precious stones and porcelain and onyx, for that it is a hard dense stone, whereon neither iron nor steel hath effect, neither can we cut off aught therefrom nor break it, save by means of the leadstone.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Marco Polo (III, xix) records more definitely this ancient belief:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Moreover in those mountains great serpents are rife to a marvelous degree, besides other vermin, and this owing to the great heat. The serpents are also the most venomous in existence, insomuch that any one going to that region runs fearful peril; for many have been destroyed by these evil reptiles.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Now among these mountains there are certain great and deep valleys, to the bottom of which there is no access. Wherefore the men who go in search of the diamonds take with them pieces of flesh, as lean as they can get, and these they cast into the bottom of a valley. Now there are numbers of white eagles that haunt those mountains and feed upon the serpents. When the eagles see the meat thrown down they pounce upon it and carry it up to some rocky hill-top where they begin to rend it. But there are men on the watch, and as soon as they see that the eagles have settled they raise a loud shouting to drive them away. And when the eagles are thus frightened away the men recover the pieces of meat, and find them full of diamonds which have stuck to the meat down in the bottom. For the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-236" data-page="236" data-folio="226" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n235_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 226">[p. 226]</button> abundance of diamonds down there in the depth of the valley is astonishing, but nobody can get them; and if one could it would be only to be incontinently devoured by the serpents which are so rife there.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The part played by the eagles is that of other sacred birds, for the defence and profit of man. Compare the bird Jaṭāyu, who gave his life in defence of Sītā against the Raksha Rāvana, in the <em>Rāmāyana</em>; the ibis at Buto who defended Egypt against the frankincense-serpents, (p. 132), and the eagles who fought the dragons. (Virgil, <em>Aeneid</em>, XI, 755; Pliny, X, 5.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Connected with these beliefs was that in the efficacy of the diamond in warding off from the wearer all sorts of evils. “Sir John Mandeville” (<em>Travels</em>, XVII), recounts it for his day, and it may still be observed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“He that beareth the diamond upon him, it giveth him hardiness and manhood, and it keepeth the limbs of his body whole. It giveth him victory of his enemies in plea and in war, if his cause be rightful. .  .  . And if any cursed witch or enchanter should bewitch him, all that sorrow and mischance shall turn to himself through virtue of that stone. And no wild beast dare assail the man that beareth it on him. And it healeth him that is lunatic, and them that the fiend pursueth or travaileth. And if venom or poison be brought in presence of the diamond, anon it beginneth to wax moist and for to sweat. . . Nathles it befalleth often time that the good diamond loseth his virtue by sin, and for incontinence of him that beareth it. And then it is needful to make it to recover his virtue again, or else it is of little value.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><strong>56. Sapphires.</strong>—The text is <em>hyakinthos</em>, which has been translated as jacinth, ruby and amethyst. Jacinth is a product of Africa rather than India. Rubies are from Burma and probably never came in great quantities from India. Pliny says that the hyacinth resembles the amethyst, but draws a distinction between them. Pliny probably had in mind a violet sapphire, and his word really might be translated as meaning all tints of sapphire from blue to purple.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dionysius Periegetes refers to the “lovely land of the Indians where the complexions of the dwellers are dark, their limbs exquisitely sleek and smooth, and the hair of their heads surpassing smooth and dark blue like the hyacinth.” (McCrindle, <em>Ancient India</em>, p. 188.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">W. Goodchild (<em>Precious Stones</em>, p. 183), also thinks that the sapphire was the <em>hyacinthus</em> of Pliny, and says that the principal source of sapphires in that part of the world was in the watered gravels of Southern Ceylon, which were derived from watered crystaline rocks; and at the time of the Periplus the natural market would have been on the Malabar coast. The ruby, which is practically of the same <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-237" data-page="237" data-folio="227" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n236_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 227">[p. 227]</button> chemical composition, being of the corundum group, was found in the same place as the sapphire in Ceylon, and was probably classified by Pliny under the <em>carbunculus</em> (XXXVII, 25). Both rubies and sapphires are found in much greater quantities in Burma and Siam, but at the time of the Periplus these deposits were probably unknown to western commerce.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">56. <strong>Tortoise-shell from Chryse.</strong>—Fabricius objects to this reading, and alters it to “that found along the coast;” but it is probable that the text gives a correct reference to the active trade of Eastern shipping in South Indian ports; which is, indeed, specifically mentioned in §§ 60 and 63. Marco Polo notes particularly the ships “from the great province of Manzi,” and says (III, xxv) that the ships from Malabar to Aden and Egypt “are not one to ten of those that go to the eastward; a very notable fact.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To assume that conditions were the same at the time of the Periplus would be to go beyond the evidence; yet the records of the Chinese themselves point strongly to the existence of an active sea-trade at that time, certainly to Malacca, and less frequently, perhaps, to India and beyond.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">With this item ends the list of articles traded in by the author of the Periplus. It is interesting to compare it with the letter from the Zamorin of Calicut to the King of Portugal, carried by Vasco da Gama on his return from India fourteen centuries later: “In my kingdom there is abundance of cinnamon, cloves, ginger, pepper, and precious stones. What I seek from thy country is gold, silver, coral, and scarlet.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">57. <strong>Hippalus first discovered.</strong>—The discovery of Hippalus, which may be placed at about 45 A. D. (see p. 8), opened a new ocean to Roman shipping; but it is probable that Arabian and Dravidian craft had frequented that ocean for many centuries, and inconceivable that they should not have made use of the periodic changes of the monsoons, by far the most notable feature of their climate. The evidence of both countries indicates, on the contrary, that they steered boldly out of sight of land, before records were written to tell of it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Kennedy in an article in the <em>Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society</em>, 1898, (pp. 248-287) also thinks that the monsoons were understood before the time of Hippalus, but doubts the beginning of any regular sea-trade before the beginning of the 7th century B. C., ascribing all such trade to the activities of Nabonidus, in whose time ships were known to have come to Babylon from India and even from China. Following this reign he thinks sea-trade between India and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-238" data-page="238" data-folio="228" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n237_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 228">[p. 228]</button> Babylon flourished for a couple of centuries, being mainly Dravidian but partly Aryan, and leading to the settlement of Indian traders in Arabia, East Africa, Babylonia and China. He minimizes the importance of the early Egyptian trading-voyages, considering them purely local, while the numerous references to articles and routes of early trade in the Hebrew scriptures he passes by with the assertion that they are due to the revision following the return of Ezra.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But whatever may have been Ezra’s revision of the Hebrew books, substantially the same articles of trade are described in the records of Egypt at corresponding dates, and they indicate a trade in articles of Indian origin to the Somali coast and overland to the Nile, centuries before Ezra’s day. (See also under §§ 6, 10, 11, and 12.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Such opinions presume a continuous trading-journey without exchange of cargoes at common meeting-points. But primitive trade passes from tribe to tribe and port to port. At the time of the Periplus cargoes changed hands in Malacca, Malabar, Somaliland, South Arabia, Adulis and Berenice. The custom is stated in detail in the Deir el Bahri reliefs describing Queen Hatshepsut’s expedition of 1500 B. C., where Amon-Re tells the queen,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“No one trod the incense-terraces, which the people knew not; they were heard of from mouth to mouth by hearsay of the ancestors. The marvels brought thence under thy fathers, the Kings of Lower Egypt, <em>were brought from one to another</em>, and since the time of the ancestors of the Kings of Upper Egypt, who were of old, <em>as a return for many payments</em>.” (Breasted, <em>Ancient Records</em>, II, 287).</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It was the particular achievement of the Egyptian Punt expeditions that they traced the treasured articles to their source and freed the land from the heavy charge of those “many payments.” Likewise Hippalus must be remembered, not for a discovery new to the world, but for freeing the Roman Empire from Arabian monopoly of the Eastern trade by tracing it to its source. Beyond India no lasting discovery was made. Ptolemy, indeed, knew of Cattigara through the account given by Marinus of Tyre; but such voyages were exceptional, and the majority of the Chinese ships stopped at Malacca, while the Malay <em>colandia</em> carried the trade to Malabar. It remained for the Arabs to complete the “through line” by opening direct communication under the Bagdad Caliphate, between the ends of the earth, Lisbon and Canton.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Prof. T. W. Rhys Davids, in the <em>Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society</em>, 1899, p. 432, quotes an interesting Buddhist passage referring to early sea-trade as follows: <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-239" data-page="239" data-folio="229" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n238_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 229">[p. 229]</button> “In the Dialogues of the Buddha is a passage in the Kevaddha Sutta of Digha—5th cent. B. C. The Buddha says:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Long ago ocean-going merchants were wont to plunge forth upon the sea, on board a ship, taking with them a shoresighting bird. When the ship was out of sight of land they would set the shoresighting bird free. And it would go to the East and to the South and to the West and to the North, and to the intermediate points, and rise aloft. If on the horizon it caught sight of land, thither it would go, but if not it would come back to the ship again. Just so, brother,” etc.</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cosmas Indicopleustes found this same custom in Ceylon in the 6th century A. D., merchants depending on shoresighting birds instead of observations of the sun or stars.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There are similar passages in the oldest of the Vedas (see Gibson’s <em>Rig Veda</em>, Vol. I):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Varuna, who knows the path of the birds flying through the air, he, abiding in the ocean, knows also the course of ships.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“May Ushas dawn today, the excitress of chariots which are harnessed at her coming, as those who are desirous of wealth send ships to sea.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“Do thou, Agni, whose countenance is turned to all sides, send off our adversaries, as if in a ship to the opposite shore. Do thou convey us in a ship across the sea for our welfare.” (A remarkable prayer for safe conduct at sea.)</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Kālidāsa, in the <em>Sakuntalā</em>, gives the story of the merchant Dhanavriddhi, whose immense wealth devolved to the king on the former’s perishing at sea and leaving no heirs behind him.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Hitopadesa</em> describes a ship as a necessary requisite for a man to traverse the ocean, and a story is given of a certain merchant, “who, after having been twelve years on his voyage, at last returned home with a cargo of precious stones.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Institutes of Manu include rules for the guidance of maritime commerce.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The passages quoted above indicate a well-developed and not a primitive trade. The sea-trade was principally of Dravidian development, while both the Vedas and the Buddhist writings are of Aryan origin, and refer to things new to their race but old in the world.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(See also Bühler, <em>Indische Studien</em>, in <em>Sitzungsberichte der Kais. Akad. d. Wissenschaften</em>, Vienna, 1895, No. 3, pp. 81-2; <em>Indian Palæography</em>, § 5; Foulke, in <em>Indian Antiquary</em>, XVI, 7; Lassen, III, 3.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">More significant is the Phœnician origin of the Dravidian alphabet, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-240" data-page="240" data-folio="230" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n239_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 230">[p. 230]</button> long before the Aryan invasion of southern India; while a passage in the <em>Rāmāyana</em> suggests the ships of those whom the invaders contemptuously called “monkeys.” When Rāma was dispatching his messengers to the four winds in search of Sītā, it was the maligned Hanumān who “flew” across the Gulf of Manār to Ceylon and discovered her. Who can doubt that the wings he used were sails, or that the Dravidians ferried across to Ceylon a force of Aryan landsmen, who later turned and crushed them under the caste-system and established the dynasties of <em>Drāviḍa-dēsam?</em> Stern must have been the subjection that brought them to worship one of their own race under the guise of a monkey, and to carry the cult of the monkey-god Hanumān in their own ships to the vales of Oman, where monkeys are unknown and where it has outlived the memory of its founders, to the confusion of the modern observer. (Gen. S. B. Miles, in <em>Geographical Journal</em>, VII, 336.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Significant also is the fact that Lieutenant Speke, when planning his discovery of the source of the Nile, secured his best information from a map reconstructed out of the <em>Purānas</em>. (<em>Journal</em>, pp. 27, 77, 216; Wilford, in <em>Asiatic Researches</em>, III). It traced the course of the river, the “Great Krishna,” through <em>Cusha-dvîpa</em>, from a great lake in <em>Chandristhān</em>, “Country of the Moon,” which it gave the correct position in relation to the Zanzibar islands. The name was from the native <em>Unya-muézi</em>, having the same meaning; and the map correctly mentioned another native name, <em>Amara</em>, applied to the district bordering Lake Victoria Nyanza.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“All our previous information,” says Speke, “concerning the hydrography of these regions, originated with the ancient Hindus, who told it to the priests of the Nile; and all those busy Egyptian geographers, who disseminated <em>their</em> knowledge with a view to be famous for <em>their</em> long-sightedness, in solving the mystery which enshrouded the source of their holy river, were so many hypothetical humbugs.
