In 1888 the spinners who supplied the finest quality were said to be reduced to two elderly women in the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-268" data-page="268" data-folio="258" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n267_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 258">[p. 258]</button> village of Dhamrai, about 20 miles north of Dacca, but it was thought that the industry might be revived with any revival of the demand for this fine fabric.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">An incredible amount of patience and skill were required in this industry. One way of testing the fineness of the fabric, often described by mediæval and earlier travelers, was to pass a whole piece of 20 yards long and 1 yard wide through an ordinary finger-ring. The best test, however, was by the weight in proportion to size and number of threads. It is said that 200 years ago a piece of muslin 15 yards long by 1 yard wide could be made so fine as to weigh only 900 grains, or a little over 1-10 of a pound. In 1840 a piece of the same dimensions and texture could not be made finer than 1,600 grains and was valued at about $50. A piece of this muslin 10 yards long by 1 yard wide could not be woven in less than five months, and the work could only be carried on in the rainy season when the moisture in the air would prevent the thread from breaking.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">At several places in northwestern India fine muslins were produced, but nowhere of quality equal to those of Bengal. These also were shipped westward, appearing in the Periplus as exports at the mouth of the Indus and at the Gulf of Cambay. The change from hand spinning and weaving to power looms and spindles was not gradual as in Europe, but was due to the direct importation of European fabrics, so that a few months sufficed to destroy the earlier industry and to lay the way for the modern textile mills of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(See Henry Lee, <em>The Vegetable Lamb of Tartary</em>. J. H. Furneaux, <em>India: Bombay</em>, 1899; chap. iii. T. N. Mukharji, <em>Art Manufac- tures of India</em>. Also, <em>The Cotton Plant</em>, published by the U. S. Department of Agriculture, 1896.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">63. <strong>Gold mines.</strong>—This was probably the gold of the Chota Nagpur plateau, located from 75 to 150 miles west of the Ganges mouth. The rivers flowing north and east of these highlands have long produced alluvial gold in considerable quantities. The river Sōn, which formerly flowed into the Ganges at the site of the ancient capital <em>Pātaliputra</em>, the modern Patna, was called by the classical writers <em>Erannoboas</em>, from the Sanscrit <em>hiranya-vaha</em>, “carrying gold.” (McCrindle, <em>Ancient India</em>, p. 43; <em>cf.</em> the Aurannoboas of § 53.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There was also a substantial supply from Tibet, which produced the famous “ant-gold” mentioned by all the classical writers from Herodotus to Pliny. As Ball pointed out (<em>Journal of the Royal Irish Academy</em>, June, 1884), the “ant-gold” was a Sanscrit name for the small fragments of alluvial gold; this name was passed on, being applied to the dogs of the Tibetan miners, which were also referred to as <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-269" data-page="269" data-folio="259" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n268_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 259">[p. 259]</button> griffins. The ‘‘horn of the gold-digging ant,’’ mentioned by Pliny as preserved in the temple of Hercules at Erythræ, was a gold-miner’s pick-axe, made of a wild sheep’s horn mounted on a handle. (See Herodotus III, 102-5; Arrian, <em>Anabasis</em> V, 4-7; Strabo, XV, i, 44; Pliny, XI, 36; McCrindle, <em>Ancient India</em>, 51.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gold was also brought into India through the Tipperah country about 60 miles east of the Ganges delta; coming chiefly from the river-washings of Assam and northern Burma.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Tavernier notes (III, xvi) that it was of poor quality, like the silk of that country, and that both were sent overland to China in exchange for silver.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In Assam, Ball notes, it was formerly the custom for the rulers to require their subjects to wash for gold a certain number of days every year, while regular gold-washers were taxed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Tipperah merchants trading in Dacca, according to Tavernier (III, xv), took back ‘‘coral, yellow amber, tortoise-shell bracelets, and others of sea shells, with numerous round and square pieces of the size of our 15 <em>sol</em> coins, which are also of the same tortoise-shell and sea-shells.’’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Assam washings are, however, of substantial yield, as Tavernier himself states (III, xvii). See also Ball, <em>Economic Geology of India</em>, p. 231, and the <em>Alamgir nama</em> of Muhammad Kazim (1663), in the <em>Indian Antiquary</em>, July, 1887.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The coin called <em>caltis</em> is thought by Benfey to be the Sanscrit <em>kalita</em>, ‘‘numbered.’’ There was, however, a South Indian coin called <em>kali</em> (Elliot, <em>op. cit.</em>, 137), while Vincent, quoting Stuckius, mentions one of Bengal called <em>kallais</em>. Wilford (<em>Asiatic Researches</em>, V, 269), preferred the refined gold called <em>canden</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny mentions gold on the Malabar coast (coming from the mines of Mysore); but, as Watt observes (p. 565), gold has always been mainly an article of import in India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">63. <strong>Chryse Island</strong> (the ‘‘golden’’).—There can be little doubt that by this was meant the Malacca peninsula, known to Ptolemy as the <em>Aurea Chersonesus</em>, although the location ‘‘just opposite the Ganges’’ disposes of a long voyage in rather summary fashion. Immense gold mines of ancient date have been discovered in the Malayan State of Pahang, north of Malacca, and these are probably the ones which gave the name of ‘‘golden’’ to the peninsula. It is known from Chinese records that ships from that country made the journey to Malacca as early as the 4th century B. C., and perhaps as early as the 12th; while the legend of Buddha’s visit to Cambodia is at least <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-270" data-page="270" data-folio="260" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n269_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 260">[p. 260]</button> suggestive of the great influence exercised from India over all Indo-China.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">H. C. Clifford (<em>Further India</em>, N. Y., 1904, pp. 6-7) gives an excellent account of the hazy, yet vaguely correct, ideas of the Romans in the 1st and 2d centuries concerning the Far East. “Of Chryse, the golden, Pliny has nothing to tell us, and the author of the Periplus tells us only that it was situated opposite to the Ganges. He speaks, however, of Thina, the land of silk, situated ‘where the seacoast ends externally,’ whence we may gather that Chryse was conceived by him as an island lying not only to the east of the Ganges, but also to the southward of the Chinese Empire. This indicates a distinct advance in knowledge, for the isle of Chryse, albeit still enveloped in a golden haze, was to the author of the Periplus a real country, and no mere mythical fairyland. Rumors must have reached him concerning it, on which he believed he could rely; and this would tend to prove that the sea-route to China via the Straits of Malacca, even though it was not yet in general use, was no longer unknown to the mariners of the east. We know that less than a century later the sailor Alexander, from whom Marinus of Tyre derived the knowledge subsequently utilized by Ptolemy, himself sailed to the Malay peninsula, and beyond, and it may safely be concluded that the feasibility of this southeastern passage had become known to the seafarers of China long before an adventurer from the west was enabled to test the fact of its existence through the means of an actual voyage.” And as illustrating the state of knowledge in the Roman world in the 1st century, Mr. Clifford aptly cites Josephus (<em>Antiquities of the Jews</em>, VIII, 2) who recounts the Ophir voyages of Solomon, venturing some curious identifications: “At Ezion-Geber, a bay of Egypt on the Erythræan Sea, the king constructed a number of ships. The port is now named Berenice(!), and is near the city of Elan, formerly deemed to be in the Hebrew jurisdiction. King Hiram greatly assisted King Solomon in preparing his navy, sending him mariners and pilots, who conducted Solomon’s officers to the land <em>that of old was called Ophir, but now the Aurea Chersonesus</em>, which belongs to India, to fetch gold.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is uncertain what knowledge Pliny had of Further India. His account of Eastern Asia (VI, 20) professes to begin with the “Scythian Ocean,”—that is, the Arctic—and after some names of doubtful origin he mentions “the Promontory of Chryse . . . and the nation of the Attacori on the gulf of that name, a people protected by their sunny hills from all noxious blasts . . . and in the interior the Caseri, a people of India, who look toward the Scythians, and eat human flesh. Here are also numerous wandering nomad tribes of India.”</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-271"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="271" data-folio="261" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n270_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 261">[p. 261]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The numerous migrations from India into Indo-China, both before and after the Christian era, give ample ground for the belief that the ports of South India and Ceylon were in truth, as the Periplus states, the center of an active trade with the Far East, employing larger ships, and in greater number, than those coming from Egypt.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The great migration from Gujarāt to Java in the 6th century A. D., and the resulting Hindu kingdoms, have already been referred to, and their greatest monuments remain to us in the tremendous Buddhist temples of Boroboedor and Brambanan. If Clifford’s belief is correct, the ruins at Angkor-Wat in Cambodia are no less distinctively of ancient Hindu origin. Of these he quotes François Garnier: “Perhaps never, in any place, has a more imposing mass of stone been raised with more art and science. If we wonder at the Pyramids as a gigantic achievement of human strength and patience, then to a strength and patience no whit less here we must add genius!”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>A Land called This.</strong>—This can hardly be other than the great western state of China, Ts’in, and “the city called Thinæ” (meant, probably, as the genitive of This), was its capital, Hién-yang, later known as Si-gnan-fu, on the Wei river not far above its confluence with the Hoang-ho, in the present province of Shen-si. This state of Ts’in was for centuries the most powerful of the Chinese states, and a constant menace to the imperial power. The Chóu dynasty, which ruled from 867 to 255 B. C., found itself harassed in the west by the Tartar tribes, and in the east by rebellious subjects, the states of Weï, Han, Chau, Ts’i and Ch’u. Very early in the dynasty, perhaps in the 8th century B. C., a portion of their sovereign rights were resigned to the prince of Ts’in, in consideration of his undertaking the defence of the frontier against the Tartars. This policy naturally profited Ts’in more than the empire, and the princes of Ts’in, as the annals put it, “like wolves or tigers wished to draw all the other princes into their claws, so that they might devour them.” The power of Ts’in grew until it overbalanced the confederation of eastern states, and the imperial power itself. As Tartar territory was conquered it was incorporated into the Ts’in dominions, and finally a Ts’in prince became Emperor of China in 255 B. C. The greatest of the Ts’in monarchs, Ts’in Chi Hwangti, who ruled from 221 to 209 B. C., is one of the brighest names in Chinese history. It was he who began the Great Wall, and who pushed the Chinese frontier across the Gobi desert, making Hami, under the Tian-Shan mountains, his outpost, and thus preparing the way for direct communication with Bactria. Regular caravan travel between China and Bactria is said to have begun in 188 B. C.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-272"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="272" data-folio="262" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n271_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 262">[p. 262]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But the success of Ts’in had brought its own reaction. It was itself so much a Tartar state that it could not control all China, and it gave way to the Han dynasty. The political importance of the state was emphasized, however, by the first Han emperor, Kaotsou, who removed his capital from Loyang in Honan to Hién-Yang or Singanfu in Shensi, the ancient Ts’in capital, and in order to make that western location more accessible to the rest of the empire, built a great high-road from Loyang to Singanfu, which is still in use.</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-028"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-028_p0272.webp" alt="Buddhist pilgrim in northwestern China: from a 6-ft. panel in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia, 1128 times enlarged from a portion of a film exposed by Bailey Willis, Carnegie Institution, Washington." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Buddhist pilgrim in northwestern China: from a 6-ft. panel in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia, 1128 times enlarged from a portion of a film exposed by Bailey Willis, Carnegie Institution, Washington.</figcaption></figure><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-273"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="273" data-folio="263" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n272_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 263">[p. 263]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Han dynasty soon lost its outposts beyond the wall, and made no effort to recover them until the reign of Kwang Vouti, 25-58 A. D., who made China a military power and conquered Anam, and by his policy toward the Yueh-chi reasserted sovereignty over Turkestan. His son, Mingti, began the aggressive westward policy which led to the great conquests of the General Pan-chao, who led his army of Chinese and Tartars as far as the Caspian, and who defeated near Khotan the Yueh-chi king Kadphises, then established in upper India. It was in this region that Buddhism seems first to have reached China, rather than through Tibet or Burma, and from this time China was always more or less directly in communication with Western Asia.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(See Hirth, <em>Ancient History of China;</em>—Richard, <em>Comprehensive Geography of the Chinese Empire;</em>—Douglas, <em>China;</em>—Boulger, <em>History of China;</em>—E. H. Parker, <em>China;</em>—H. B. Morse, <em>The Trade and Administration of the Chinese Empire.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>Raw silk and silk yarn and silk cloth.</strong>—See also under §§ 39, 49 and 56. This is the earliest correct statement of the source of silk and of the routes by which it reached the world’s markets.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Silk is the cocoon-secretion of the mulberry-leaf moth, <em>Bombyx mori</em>, family <em>Bombycidæ</em>, order <em>Lepidoptera;</em> native, apparently, and first cultivated, in the warm-temperate climate of northwestern China.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Chinese legends mention the making of musical instruments of wood, with silk threads, under the emperor Fu-hi, (29th century B. C.), while the rearing of the worms and the invention of reel, loom, etc., are ascribed to Leï-tsu, known as the “Lady of Si-ling,” wife of the emperor Huang-ti (27th century B. C.). Cloth was woven of silk, embroidered by the empress, and those of the higher classes were enabled to discard skins as wearing apparel. Soon other textile materials were discovered, and dyeing introduced; so that rank and position were for the first time indicated by the man’s outward appearance.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the <em>Chóu-li</em>, dating from the 11th century B. C., it appears that the Chinese government supervised the production of silk in every detail, and that specialties of design, ornament, and embroidery, were already monopolized in different families. The same book describes the provinces of China: King-chóu, the modern Hu-nan, had a trade in cinnabar, ivory, and skins; Yu-chóu, next on the north and reaching the Yellow River, traded in bamboos, varnish, silk and hemp; while the northernmost, Ping-chóu (the modern Shan-si) was noted especially for cotton and silk textures. It was this province which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-274" data-page="274" data-folio="264" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n273_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 264">[p. 264]</button> was most in contact with the nomad tribes of Central Asia, through whose hands silk first reached the western nations. (Hirth, <em>Ancient History of China</em>, 9, 22-3, 117, 121-2).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The antiquity of the silk industry in India is uncertain, but the weight of evidence seems to be in favor of its importation from China, by way of the Brahmaputra valley, Assam and Eastern Bengal, early in the Christian era; while the cultivation of native varieties, not feeding on mulberry leaves—the <em>Saturnidæ</em>, including <em>Antheræa paphia</em> (the modern tasar silk); <em>Antheræa assama</em> (feeding on laurel species principally); and <em>Attacus ricini</em> (feeding on the castor-oil plant) were probably all stimulated by the value of the <em>Bombyx silk</em>. (See Watt, pp. 992-1026; <em>Cambridge Natural History</em>, VI, 375.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The trade in silk yarn and silk cloth existed in Northern India soon after the Aryan invasion. Silk is mentioned several times, as gifts from foreign countries, in the <em>Mahābhārata</em>, the <em>Rāmāyana</em>, and the Institutes of Manu; and it may be assumed that some trade at least went farther west. The Egyptian records do not mention it prior to the Persian conquest, and it was, no doubt, through the empires of Darius and Xerxes that it first reached the Mediterranean world.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Hebrew scriptures contain at least two references to silk: the <em>dmeshek</em> of Amos III, 12 seems to be the Arabic <em>dimaks</em>, English <em>damask</em>, a silken fabric; while <em>meshi</em> in Ezekiel XVI, 10 seems to mean a silken gauze. Isaiah also (XLIX, 12) mentions the <em>Sinim</em> in a manner indicating extreme distance.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It has been supposed that the Greeks learned of silk through Alexander’s expedition, but it probably reached them previously through Persia. Aristotle (<em>Hist. Anim.</em>, V, xix, 11) gives a reasonably correct account: “It is a great worm which has horns and so differs from others. At its first metamorphosis it produces a caterpillar, then a bombylius, and lastly a chrysalis—all these changes taking place within six months. From this animal women separate and reel off the cocoons and afterwards spin them. It is said that this was first spun in the island of Cos by Pamphile, daughter of Plates.” This indicates a steady importation of raw silk on bobbins before Aristotle’s time. The fabric he mentions was the famous <em>Coa vestis</em>, or transparent gauze (woven also at Tyre and elsewhere in Syria), which came into favor in the time of Cæsar and Augustus. Pliny mentions Pamphile of Cos, “who discovered the art of unwinding the silk” (from the bobbins, not from the cocoons) “and spinning a tissue therefrom; indeed, she ought not to be deprived of the glory of having discovered the art of making garments which, while they cover a woman, at the same time reveal her naked charms.” (XI, 26).</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-275"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="275" data-folio="265" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n274_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 265">[p. 265]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He refers to the same fabric in VI, 20, where he speaks of “the Seres, so famous for the wool that is found in their forests. After steeping it in water, they comb off a soft down that adheres to the leaves; and then to the females of our part of the world they give the twofold task of unraveling their textures, and of weaving the threads afresh. So manifold is the labor, and so distant are the regions which are thus ransacked to supply a dress through which our ladies may in public display their charms.” Compare Lucan, <em>Pharsalia</em>, X, 141, who describes Cleopatra, “her white breasts resplendent through the Sidonian fabric, which, wrought in close texture by the skill of the Seres, the needle of the workman of the Nile has separated, and has loosened the warp by stretching out the web.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Silk fabrics of this kind were much affected by men also during the reign of Augustus, but the fashion was considered effeminate, and early in the reign of Tiberius the Roman Senate enacted a law “that men should not defile themselves by wearing garments of silk.” (Tacitus, <em>Annals</em>, II, 33.) The cost was enormously high; from an account of the Emperor Aurelian we learn that silk was worth its weight in gold, and that he neither used it himself nor allowed his wife to possess a garment of it, thereby setting an example against the luxurious tastes that were draining the empire of its resources.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny includes it in his list of the “most valuable productions” (XXXVII, 67); “the most costly things that are gathered from trees are nard and Seric tissues.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny (XXI, 8) speaks of other uses for silk: “Luxury arose at last to such a pitch that a chaplet was held in no esteem at all if it did not consist entirely of leaves sewn together with the needle. More recently again they have been imported from India, or from nations beyond the countries of India. But it is looked upon as the most refined of all, to present chaplets made of nard leaves, or else of silk of many colors steeped in unguents. Such is the pitch to which the luxuriousness of our women has at last arrived!”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Among both Greek and Roman writers there was some confusion between cotton and silk, both being called “tree wool;” and Fabricius, in his translation of the Periplus, omits silk altogether, considering raw material, yarn and cloth alike to be Turkestan cotton. But although these accounts err in some details, Pliny is sufficiently correct in his description of cotton. He distinguishes the wool-bearing trees of the Seres from those of the Indians (XIV, 4), and describes the cotton shrub, with its “fruit resembling a bearded nut, containing on the inside a silky down, which is spun into threads; the tissue made from which is superior to all others in whiteness and softness” (XIX, 2), <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-276" data-page="276" data-folio="266" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n275_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 266">[p. 266]</button> while his account of the silkworm is at least within sight of the truth, although not so near it as Aristotle’s:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“At first they assume the appearance of small butterflies with naked bodies, but soon after, being unable to endure the cold, they throw out bristly hairs, and assume quite a thick coat against the winter by rubbing off the down that covers the leaves, by the aid of the roughness of their feet. This they compress into balls by carding it with their claws, and then draw it out and hang it between the branches of the trees, making it fine by combing it out as it were; last of all, they take and roll it round their body, thus forming a nest in which they are enveloped. It is in this state that they are taken; after which they are placed in earthen vessels in a warm place, and fed upon bran. A peculiar sort of down soon shoots forth upon the body, on being clothed with which they are sent to work upon another task. The cocoons which they have begun to form are rendered soft and pliable by the aid of water, and are then drawn out into threads by means of a spindle made of a reed. Nor, in fact, have the men even felt ashamed to make use of garments formed of this material in consequence of their extreme lightness in summer; for so greatly have manners degenerated in our own day that so far from wearing a cuirass, a garment even is found to be too heavy.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(See also Lassen, I, 317-322; III, 25; Yates, <em>Textrinum An- tiquorum</em>.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The reeling of silk from the cocoons was confused into a combing of down from the leaves, which had also a basis of truth, but was the cause of the confusion with cotton. Compare Virgil, <em>Georgics</em>, II, 121;—“Velleraque ut foliis depectant tenuia Seres.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny finally distinguishes between the two fibers in referring to Arabian cotton (XII, 21): “trees that bear wool, but of a different nature from those of the Seres; as in these trees the <em>leaves</em> produce nothing at all, and indeed might very readily be taken for those of the vine.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The word “silk” is from a Mongolian original, <em>sirkek</em>, meaning silk; Korean <em>sir</em>, Chinese <em>ssi</em>. Hence the Greek <em>sēr</em>, Latin <em>sericum</em>. From this word the name <em>Seres</em> was applied to the peoples through whose hands the product came; by which must be understood, not the Chinese themselves, but rather the Turkish or Tibetan intermediaries. That the word was loosely extended to cover most of Eastern Asia is undeniable; but Ptolemy distinguishes the <em>Sinæ</em>, Isaiah the <em>Sinim</em>, while the Periplus gives nearly the correct form, <em>This</em>, for China proper.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pliny has a curious mixture of Seres and Cirrhadæ in his <em>Scyritæ</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-277" data-page="277" data-folio="267" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n276_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 267">[p. 267]</button> (VII, 2), whose flat-nosed Mongolian faces he describes as having “merely holes in their faces instead of nostrils,” and whom he connects with an allied race, the Astomi, “a people who have no mouths, who live on the eastern side of India, near the source of the Ganges; their bodies are rough and hairy, and they cover themselves with a down plucked from the leaves of trees.” Here he shows some knowledge of the silk trade through Assam.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Ammianus Marcellinus (XXIII, vi) has more knowledge of the Seres:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. “Beyond the districts of the two Scythias, on the eastern side, is a ring of mountains which surround Serica, a country considerable both for its extent and the fertility of its soil. This tribe on their western side border on the Scythians, on the north and the east they look toward snowy deserts; toward the south they extend as far as India and the Ganges. . . .</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">67. “The Seres themselves live quietly, always avoiding arms and battles; and as ease is pleasant to moderate and quiet men, they give trouble to none of their neighbors. Their climate is agreeable and healthy; the sky serene, the breezes gentle and delicious. They have numbers of shining groves, the trees of which through continued watering produce a crop like the fleece of a sheep, which the natives make into a delicate wool, and spin into a kind of fine cloth, formerly confined to the use of the nobles, but now procurable by the lowest of the people without distinction.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">68. “The natives themselves are the most frugal of men, cultivating a peaceful life, and shunning the society of other men. And when strangers cross their river to buy their cloth, or any other of their merchandise, they interchange no conversation, but settle the price of the articles wanted by nods and signs; and they are so modest that, while selling their own produce, they never buy any foreign wares.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But to the Græco-Roman world the Seres were a people as ubiquitous as the subjects of Prester John in the middle ages. The Chēras of Malabar (<em>Seri</em> in Sinhalese mouths; see p. 209), and even Ausar and Masira in Southern Arabia (see p. 140) were identified with them.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Concerning the long struggles of the emperors at Constantinople with the Sassanid monarchs in Persia, over the ever-increasing silk-trade, culminating in the romantic success of the Christian monks who succeeded in bringing the jealously-guarded eggs to Justinian, hidden in a bamboo cane, thereby laying the foundation of the silkculture of Greece and the Levant, see Beazley, <em>Dawn of Modern</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-278" data-page="278" data-folio="268" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n277_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 268">[p. 268]</button> <em>Geography</em>, Vol. I;—Heyd, <em>Histoire du Commerce du Levant au Moyen Age</em>;—D’Anville, <em>Recherches géographiques et historiques sur la Sérique des anciens</em> (1768) in <em>Mémoires de l’ Académie Royale des Inscriptions et Belles- Lettres</em>, xxxii, 573-603;—Reinaud, <em>Relations politiques et commerciales de l’ Empire Romain avec l’ Asie Orientale pendant les cinq premiers siècles de l’ère chrétienne</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">See also Richthofen, <em>China</em>, I, chap. x;—Stein, <em>Sand-buried Ruins of Khotan</em>;—Götz, <em>Verkehrswege im Dienste des Welthandels</em>, 496-511;—Speck, <em>Handelsgeschichte des Altertums</em>, I;—Letourneau, <em>L’Evolution du Commerce</em>;—Noèl, <em>Histoire du Commerce du Monde</em>, I; —Lindsay, <em>History of Merchant Shipping and Ancient Commerce</em>, I;— Mayr, <em>Lehrbuch der Handelsgeschichte</em>, I, § 16;—Tozer, <em>A History of Ancient Geography</em>, 281;—Bunbury, <em>History of Ancient Geography</em>, I, 565; II, 166, 658;—Edmunds, <em>Buddhist and Christian Gospels</em>, 4th ed., introduction.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64.  <strong>Through Bactria to Barygaza.</strong>—The overland travel from the Yellow River to Bactra, first instituted, possibly, early in the 2d century B. C. and then obstructed for nearly two centuries, followed two routes.  The earlier, and to the Chinese the most important because it led to the Khotan jade-field, was the <em>Nan-lu</em> or “southern way,” the stages of which may be traced on the map as follows:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Singanfu, Lanchowfu, Kanchow, Yümenhsien, Ansichow, Lop Nor to Tsiemo (the <em>Asmiræa</em> of the Greeks) where the routes divided. The <em>Nan-lu</em> followed south of the Tarim River to Khotan and Yarkand, thence over the Pamirs and westward to the Oxus and Bactra. This was the earliest route opened by the Chinese army under Pan Chao, being cleared in 74 A. D.  The second route, the <em>Péi-lu</em> or “northern way,” followed the same course from Singanfu to Tsiemo, thence north of the Tarim through Kuché and Aksu to Kashgar, and over the tremendous heights of the Terek to the Jaxartes and Samarcand.  Thence a route led southward to Bactra, while another led southwestward more directly to Antiochia Margiana (Merv.)  This second route was opened by Pan Chao in 94 A. D.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A variant of the <em>Péi-lu</em> led from Yümenhsien to Hami, Turfan and Kharachar, meeting the above route at Kuché; this was preferable in some respects, being close to the mountains, but was subjected to constant attacks by the savage Tartar tribes, Hami especially being a storm-center in the Chinese annals, and an important outpost for the defence of the main route.  Another variant led from Turfan through the Tian-shan to Urumtsi and Kuldja, thence by the Ili River and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-279" data-page="279" data-folio="269" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n278_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 269">[p. 269]</button> north of the mountains to Tashkend, Bokhara and Merv. This did not become important until later.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The general topography of these Turkestan routes is shown by a passage from the Han Annals quoted by Richthofen (<em>China</em>, I, 460) from Stanislaus Julien (<em>Notices sur les pays et les peuples etrangers, tirées des géographes et des historiens chinois</em>, in <em>Journal Asiatique</em>, Ser. IV, Vol. VIII, 1846, pp. 228-252), as follows:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“<em>Hsi-yü</em> is bounded on the east by the barriers of <em>Yümen-kwan</em> and <em>Yang-kwan</em>, and on the west by the <em>Tsung-ling</em> (Pamirs). But the <em>Tsung-ling</em> is the trunk from which the great mountain-ranges branch out, which enclose the district on the north and the south, and these same ranges bound the districts of <em>Nan-lu</em> and <em>Péi-lu</em> on the south and north.” And again: “The land along the <em>Nan-shan</em> (Kuen-lun) is called <em>Nan-tau</em>, and that along the <em>Péi-shan</em> (Tian-shan) is called <em>Péi-tau</em>. Both these provinces lie to the south of <em>Péi-shan</em>. . . . <em>Hsi-yü</em> extends 6000 <em>li</em> from east to west, and 1000 <em>li</em> from south to north.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">That is, Tibet and Sungaria had no part in the transcontinental silk-trade in Roman times.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This Central Asian trade-route was first comprehensively described by Marinus of Tyre, some two generations later than the Periplus. His account is preserved by Ptolemy, and is said to be based on the notes of a Macedonian silk-merchant named Maès, whose Roman name was Titianus; who did not perform the whole journey, but repeats what he learned of Turkestan from his “agents” or trading associates whom he met at the Pamirs. The route, he says, began at the Bay of Issus in Cilicia, crossed Mesopotamia, Assyria and Media, to Ecbatana and the Caspian Pass; through Parthia and Hyrcania, to Antiochia Margiana (Merv); thence through Aria into Bactria. Thence the route passed through the mountainous country of the Cōmdi, and through the territory of the Sacæ to the “Stone Tower,” the station of those merchants who trade with the Seres (Tashkurghan, in Sarikol, on the upper Yarkand River in the Chinese Pamirs; a fortified town built on a great rocky crag that rises from the Taghdumbash valley, at the convergence of routes from the Oxus, the Indus and the Yarkand. See Stein, <em>op. cit.</em>, 67-8.) Thence to the Casii (Kashgar) and through the country of the Thaguri, until after a seven-months’ journey from the “Stone Tower” the merchants arrived at “Sera Metropolis,” the “’City called Thinæ” of the Periplus.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By too literal an application of this “seven-months’ journey” both Marinus and Ptolemy were led into grave error as to the longitudinal <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-280" data-page="280" data-folio="270" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n279_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 270">[p. 270]</button> extension of Asia; but the evidence of direct trade between Rome and China is remarkable.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The first part of the route was minutely described before our author’s time, in the <em>Mansiones Parthicæ</em> of Isidorus of Charax Spasini.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This route of Maës the Macedonian followed very nearly the same direction as the Chinese <em>Nan-lu</em>, after leaving Bactra, crossing the Pamirs diagonally to Kashgar, on the <em>Péi-lu</em>, but then turning southward through Yarkand to Khotan, and in passing “Thagura” took a more southerly, and also a more direct route than the <em>Nan-lu</em> itself, which it joined half-way between Lop Nor and the Bulunzir (the “river of the Hiong-nu”); east of which all three routes were identical as far as Singanfu.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(See map to face p. 500, Vol. I, of Richthofen’s <em>China</em>;—Stieler’s <em>Hand-Atlas</em>, maps 61-2;—Stanford, <em>Atlas of the Chinese Empire</em>, plates 12, 13, 19, 21;—Lansdell, <em>Chinese Central Asia</em>, Vol. II;—Stein, <em>op. cit.</em>, chap. v. and map.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">At Bactra this overland trade-route branched again, following westward through the Parthian highlands to the Euphrates, or southward to Bamian, the Cabul valley, the Khyber Pass and the Indus. From Taxila the highway of the Maurya dynasty led through the Panjāb to the capital at Palibothra, with a branch from Mathurā southward to Ozene and the Deccan. The route down the Indus to its mouth was less important owing to the character of the tribes living on the lower reaches. This is indicated by the text, which says far more of the products carried by the overland route to Barygaza than of those coming to <em>Barbaricum</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yet a part of the Chinese trade was, apparently, localized at the mouth of the Indus. While the valuable silk cloth went to Barygaza, the yarn, or thread, went to <em>Barbaricum</em>, where it was exchanged for a product always more highly valued in China than in India—namely, frankincense; the white incense, or <em>shehri luban</em>, which Marco Polo still found in extensive use in China under the name of “milk perfume.” This is not listed in the Periplus among the imports at other Indian ports, and evidently found its way up the Indus to Peucelaotis and Bactra, and thence to China. The silk yarn, in return, went to Arabia, where it was used in making the embroidered and silk-shot fabrics for which Arabia and Syria were so famous in the Roman market.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Concerning the frankincense of the Deir-el-Bahri reliefs Mr. R. E. Drake-Brockman writes again from Bulhar, Sept. 18, 1910, that the cattle shown in those reliefs are not the humped cattle peculiar to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-281" data-page="281" data-folio="271" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n280_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 271">[p. 271]</button> Somaliland (and likewise to much of East Africa, Madagascar and Western India) but the ordinary type, without humps; which are bred in Southern Arabia and Socotra.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“The cattle of these regions and in fact the whole of Gallaland and Southern Abyssinia are all the humped variety. I have travelled fairly extensively in these regions and have never seen the non-humped breed, and very much doubt if they ever existed in these dried-up parts, as the hump is to these cattle what the camel’s hump is to the camel, a sort of storehouse. Besides this, cattle are rare in Somaliland proper, and it is improbable if they ever existed in greater numbers or were exported.”</p></blockquote></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-029"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-029_p0281.webp" alt="Vase of black pottery ornamented with figures of humped cattle. From the Madagascar collection in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Vase of black pottery ornamented with figures of humped cattle. From the Madagascar collection in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This is one more proof that the Punt Expedition did not make its terminus on the Somali coast, but must have gone to the Plain of Dhofar, or possibly to the south side of Socotra, which was a dependency of Dhofar. The localization of the island <em>Pa-anch</em> of the XIIIth dynasty tale, and the incense-land <em>Panchaia</em> of Virgil, in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-282" data-page="282" data-folio="272" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n281_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 272">[p. 272]</button> Socotra, makes that an interesting possibility; but altogether the scene on the reliefs is more strongly suggestive of Dhofar, the <em>Sachalites</em> of the Periplus. (See also pp. 120, 141-2, and 218.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">See Ptolemy, I, 11-12, VI, 13;—De Guignes, <em>Sur les liaisons et le commerce des Romains avec les Tartares et les Chinois: in Mémoires de l’Académie Royale des Inscriptions et Belles-Lettres</em>, Vol. xxxii (1798) pp. 355-69;—Rémusat, <em>Remarques sur l’extension de l’Empire Chinois du côté de l’occident</em> (1825);—Lassen, I, 13-14, II, 519-660;—Yule, <em>Cathay and the Way Thither</em>;—Stein, <em>Sand-Buried Ruins of Khotan</em>;—Gen. M. R. Haig, <em>The Indus Delta Country</em>;—Richthofen, <em>China</em>, Vol. I; —Vincent, II, 573-618;—Merzbacher, <em>The Central Tian-Shan Mountains</em>;—Bonin, <em>Grandes voies commerciales de l’Asie Centrale</em>;—Manifold, <em>Recent Exploration and Economic Development in Central and Western China</em> (with map) in <em>Geographical Journal</em>, xxiii, 281-312, Mar. 1904; —Geil, <em>The Great Wall of China</em>;—Keane, <em>Asia</em>, I, chap. v. Col. M. S. Bell, in <em>Proceedings of the Royal Geographical Society</em>, 1890, describes his journey of 1887 along the entire Central Asian trade-route between Kashgar and Peking.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>To Damirica by way of the Ganges.</strong>—This was the route across the Tibetan plateau, starting in the same direction as the Turkestan routes, from Singanfu to Lanchowfu; branching here, it led to Siningfu, thence to Koko Nor, and southwestward, by Lhasa and the Chumbi Vale to Sikkim and the Ganges. The route from Lhasa by the lower Brahmaputra was little used, owing to the savage tribes inhabiting it. There were numerous other passages into India; as, for instance, a frequented route by the Arun River through Nepāl to the Ganges, or by following the upper Brahmaputra to the sacred peak of Kailās and the source of the Sutlej, or continuing through Gartok to the upper Indus. But natural conditions, as stated in § 66 of the Periplus itself, made these routes through Western Tibet almost impracticable for commerce.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This was the route which later became the great highway of Buddhist pilgrim-travel between Mongolia and Lhasa. It is best described by one of the few white men who have ever traversed it: Huc, <em>Recollections of a Journey through Tartary, Thibet and China during 1844-46.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Chinese Buddhist monk Fa-Hien spent two years in “the country of Tamalipti, the capital of which is a seaport . . . after this he embarked in a large merchant-vessel, and went floating over the sea to the southwest. It was the beginning of winter and the wind <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-283" data-page="283" data-folio="273" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n282_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 273">[p. 273]</button> was favorable; and after fourteen days, sailing day and night, they came to the country of Singhala.’’ (<em>Travels</em>, chap. xxxvii.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“To Damirica” came the eastern shipping, according to the text; that is, the Chēra backwaters were a meeting-point for the trade from the China Sea to the Gulf of Suez. Our author did not meet these vessels at Nelcynda, because the same monsoon that brought them would have taken him away.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Marco Polo tells us something of this trade in his day (III, xxv): “There is in this kingdom of Melibar a great quantity of pepper, and ginger, and cinnamon, and turbit, and of nuts of India. They also manufacture very delicate and beautiful buckrams. The ships that come from the east bring coffee in ballast. They also bring hither cloths of silk and gold, and sendels; also gold and silver, cloves and spikenard, and other fine spices.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">See Holdich, <em>Tibet the Mysterious</em>;—Rockhill, <em>The Land of the Lamas</em>;—Sven Hedin, <em>Central Asia and Tibet</em>;—Waddell, <em>Lhasa and its Mysteries</em>; Younghusband, <em>The Geographical Results of the Tibet Mission</em>, in <em>Geographical Journal</em>, xxv, 1905;—Crosby, <em>Tibet and Turkestan</em>; Candler, <em>The Unveiling of Lhasa</em>;—Landon, <em>Lhasa, and The Opening of Tibet</em>;—Sarat Chandra Das, <em>Journey to Lhasa and Central Tibet</em>;—Littledale, <em>A Journey across Tibet</em>;—Deasy, <em>In Tibet and Chinese Turkestan</em>;—Carey, <em>Adventures in Tibet</em>;—Sandberg, <em>The Exploration of Tibet</em>;—Tsybikoff, <em>Lhasa and Central Tibet</em> (Smithsonian Report, 1903);—Prjevalski, <em>Mongolia, the Tangut Country, and the Solitudes of Northern Tibet</em>;—Sherring, <em>Western Tibet and the British Borderland</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>Few men come from there, and seldom.</strong>—Until the subjugation of Turkestan by China, travel and trade overland were naturally hazardous. The routes through Tibet and upper Burma were never so actively used as those leading through the Pamirs. For this, racial and topographical reasons were alike responsible.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">See Lassen, I, 167-9;—Kemp, <em>The Face of China</em>; also, for a most useful and detailed account of a recent journey along the littletravelled Burmese route, R. F. Johnston, <em>From Peking to Mandalay</em>. Another theory, outlined by Kingsmill (<em>The Mantse and the Golden Chersonese</em>, and <em>Ancient Tibet and its Frontagers</em>, in <em>Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society</em>, China Branch, xxxv and xxxvii), and Terrien de Lacouperie (in his introduction to Colquhoun’s <em>Among the Shans</em>), locates this entire traffic in upper Burma; identifying Thinæ with <em>Theinni</em>, the Burmese form of Hsen-wi, or the Northern Shans, and with <em>Tien</em>, the name given by Marco Polo to the Chinese province of Yunnan. (See also Rocher, <em>La Province Chinoise de Yunnan</em>.) But whatever may be the relation of Ptolemy’s <em>Sinæ</em> and Cosmas’ <em>Tzinista</em> to Burma, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-284" data-page="284" data-folio="274" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n283_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 274">[p. 274]</button> it may be asserted that the Thinæ of the Periplus had nothing to do with that region. Silk was brought thence overland “through Bactria to Barygaza,” that is, by the Turkestan route. Why ignore the ancient center of the silk industry, Singanfu, to find a fancied similarity of name in a locality never important in silk production, separated <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-285" data-page="285" data-folio="275" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n284_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 275">[p. 275]</button> from the silk-route by 1000 miles of the most difficult travelling in Asia, and not certainly settled by Shan tribes until some centuries later than the Periplus? The theory is manifestly impracticable.</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-030"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-030_p0284.webp" alt="Early Chinese Buddhist 9-storied pagoda: compare illustrations of Hindu and Abyssinian types, on pp. 64-5. From a model exhibited in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia." loading="lazy"><figcaption>Early Chinese Buddhist 9-storied pagoda: compare illustrations of Hindu and Abyssinian types, on pp. 64-5. From a model exhibited in the Commercial Museum, Philadelphia.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">With the rise of the Kushan dynasty in the northwest, and their relations towards their former home on the Chinese border, it was natural that communication by the Turkestan routes should increase. While the military successes of China did not begin until 73 A. D., it is known that the Chinese Emperor Ming-ti (who ruled from 58 to 75) introduced Buddhism into China by the invitation of two Indian Sramanas, Kāsyapa Mātanga and Bhārana, who arrived in 67 A. D. (Takakusu, Introduction to his edition of I-tsing, p. xvii.) Before such an invitation there must have been considerable activity on the part of missionaries, then as now the forerunners of commerce.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The text seems to be describing conditions prior to the journey of the Sramanas in 67 A. D.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As contrasting with the knowledge, or lack of it, which the Romans displayed concerning China, the following account of Roman Syria, particularly the district of Antioch, taken from Chinese annals of almost the same date as the Periplus, is of interest. (Quoted from Hirth, <em>China and the Roman Orient.</em>):</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">ANNALS OF THE HAN DYNASTY OF CHINA</span></h2><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Chapter</span> 88</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(<em>Section “Hou-han-shu,” partly written during the 5th century A. D., and embracing the period A. D. 25 to 220</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The first detailed account of the Roman empire contained in the Chinese annals: this account describing Roman Syria and its capital Antioch, and being based on the report of the Ambassador Kan Ying, A. D. 97</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(1) The country of <em>Ta-ts’in</em> is also called <em>Lichien</em> (Li-kin) and, as being situated on the western part of the sea, <em>Hai-hsi-kuo</em>, (<em>i. e.</em> “country of the western part of the sea”). (2) Its territory amounts to several thousand <em>li</em>; (3) it contains over four hundred cities, (4) and of dependent states there are several times ten. (5) The defences of cities are made of stone. (6) The postal stations and milestones on the roads are covered with plaster. (7) There are pine and cypress trees and all kinds of other trees and plants. (8) The people are much bent on agriculture and practice the planting of trees and the rearing of silk-worms. (9) They cut the hair of their heads, (10) wear embroidered clothing, (11) and drive in small carriages covered with white canopies; (12) when going in or out they beat drums, and hoist flags, banners, and pennants. (13) The precincts of the walled cities in which they live measure over a hundred <em>li</em> in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-286" data-page="286" data-folio="276" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n285_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 276">[p. 276]</button> circumference. (14) In the city there are five palaces, ten <em>li</em> distant from each other. (15) In the palace buildings they use crystal to make pillars; vessels used in taking meals are also made. (16) The king goes to one palace a day to hear cases. After five days he has completed his round. (17) As a rule, they let a man with a bag follow the king’s carriage. Those who have some matter to submit, throw a petition into the bag. When the king arrives at the palace he examines into the rights and wrongs of the matter. (18) The official documents are under the control of thirty-six <em>chiang</em> (generals?) who conjointly discuss government affairs. (19) Their kings are not permanent rulers, but they appoint men of merit. (20) When a severe calamity visits the country, or untimely rain-storms, the king is deposed and replaced by another. The one relieved from his duties submits to his degradation without a murmur. (21) The inhabitants of that country are tall and well-proportioned, somewhat like the Chinese, whence they are called <em>Ta-ts’in</em>. (22) The country contains much gold, silver, and rare precious stones, especially the “jewel that shines at night,” the “moonshine pearl,” the <em>hsieh-chi- hsi</em>, corals, amber, glass, <em>lang-kan</em> (a kind of coral), <em>chu-tan</em> (cinnabar?), green jadestone (<em>ching-pi</em>), gold-embroidered rugs and thin silk-cloth of various colors. (23) They make gold-colored cloth and asbestos cloth. (25) They further have “fine cloth,” also called <em>Shui-yang-ts’ui</em>, (<em>i. e.</em> down of the water-sheep); it is made from the cocoons of wild silk-worms. (25) They collect all kinds of fragrant substances, the juice of which they boil into <em>su-ho</em> (storax). (26) All the rare gems of other foreign countries come from there. (27) They make coins of gold and silver. Ten units of silver are worth one of gold. (28) They traffic by sea with <em>An-hsi</em> (Parthia) and <em>T’ien-chu</em> (India), the profit of which trade is ten-fold. (29) They are honest in their transactions and there are no double prices. (30) Cereals are always cheap. The budget is based on a well-filled treasury. (31) When the embassies of neighboring countries come to their frontier, they are driven by post to the capital, and on arrival, are presented with golden money. (32) Their kings always desired to send embassies to China, but the <em>An-hsi</em> (Parthians) wished to carry on trade with them in Chinese silks, and it is for this reason that they were cut off from communication. (33) This lasted till the ninth year of the Yen-hsi period during the emperor Huan-ti’s reign (= A. D. 166) when the king of <em>Ta-ts’in</em>, <em>An-tun</em> (Marcus Aurelius Antoninus) sent an embassy who, from the frontier of <em>Jih-nan</em> (Anam) offered ivory, rhinoceros horns, and tortoise shell. From that time dates the (direct) intercourse with this country. The list of their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-287" data-page="287" data-folio="277" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n286_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 277">[p. 277]</button> tribute contained no jewels whatever, which fact throws doubt on the tradition. (34) It is said by some that in the west of this country there is the <em>Jo-shai</em> (“weak water”) and the <em>Liu-sha</em> (“flying sands, desert”) near the residence of the <em>Hsi-wang-mu</em> (“mother of the western king”), where the sun sets. (35) The <em>Ch’ien-han-shu</em> says “from <em>T’iao-chih</em> west, going over 200 days, one is near the place where the sun sets;” this does not agree with the present book. (36) Former embassies from China all returned from <em>Wu-i</em>; there were none who came as far as <em>T’iao-chih</em>. (37) It is further said that, coming from the land-road of <em>An-hsi</em> (Parthia), you make a round at sea and, taking a northern turn, come out from the western part of the sea, whence you proceed to <em>Ta-ts’in</em>. (38) The country is densely populated; every ten <em>li</em> (of a road) are marked by a <em>t’ing</em>; thirty <em>li</em> by a <em>chih</em> (resting-place). (39) One is not alarmed by robbers, but the road becomes unsafe by fierce tigers and lions who will attack passengers, and unless these be traveling in caravans of a hundred men or more, or be protected by military equipment, they may be devoured by these beasts. (40) They also say there is a flying bridge (<em>fei-chiao</em>) of several hundred <em>li</em>, by which one may cross to the countries north of the sea. (41) The articles made of rare precious stones produced in this country are sham curiosities and mostly not genuine, whence they are not (here) mentioned.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>Under the Lesser Bear</strong>—meaning far to the north (of the Himalayas). No part of China is actually so far north as to have Ursa Minor in the zenith; this would require it to be within the Arctic Circle.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64. <strong>Empty into the Ocean.</strong>—This was the belief of most of the Greek and Roman geographers. See p. 100, where the map according to Pomponius Mela shows the Caspian directly connected with the Arctic Ocean, and Lake Mæotis connected by means of the Tanais, or Don, river. So Strabo (XI, vi, 1): “The Caspian is a bay extending from the ocean to the south. At its commencement it is very narrow; as it advances further inward, and particularly toward the extremity, it widens. . . . Eratosthenes says that the navigation of this sea was known to the Greeks; that the part of the voyage along the coast of the Albanians and the Gadusii comprised 5400 stadia; and the part along the country of the Anariaci, Mardi, and Hyrcani, as far as the mouth of the river Oxus, 4800 stadia, and thence to the Jaxartes, 2400 stadia.” This passage, often ridiculed, is rather an indication of the strong probability that the Caspian and Aral Seas were joined together until after the Christian era, so that the Amu and Syr were in truth accessible to the Greek adventurers <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-288" data-page="288" data-folio="278" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n287_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 278">[p. 278]</button> from Colchis, crossing from the Euxine Sea. As to Lake Mæotis (the Sea of Azov) Strabo says (XI, i, 5): “Asia has a kind of peninsular form, surrounded on the west by the river Tanais and the Palus Mæotis as far as the Cimmerian Bosphorus, and that part of the coast of the Euxine which terminates at Colchis; on the north by the Ocean, as far as the mouth of the Caspian Sea; on the east by the same sea, as far as the confines of Armenia.” These errors were corrected by Ptolemy, but subsequently revived. See Tozer, <em>History of Ancient Geography</em>, 345, 367;—Huntington, <em>The Pulse of Asia</em>;—Mackinder, <em>The Geographical Pivot of History</em>, in <em>Geographical Journal</em>, xxiii, 422-437, April, 1904;—Kropotkin, <em>The Desic- cation of Eurasia</em>, <em>ibid.</em>, June, 1904.</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-031"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-031_p0288.webp" alt="In this group of modern Tibetans may be found all the types mentioned in the closing paragraphs of the Periplus: “the men with flattened noses,” the “Horse-faces” and the “Long-faces,” of § 62, and the “men with short, thick bodies and broad, flat faces” of § 65." loading="lazy"><figcaption>In this group of modern Tibetans may be found all the types mentioned in the closing paragraphs of the Periplus: “the men with flattened noses,” the “Horse-faces” and the “Long-faces,” of § 62, and the “men with short, thick bodies and broad, flat faces” of § 65.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. <strong>Besatæ.</strong>—These were another Tibeto-Burman tribe, allied to the Cirrhadæ, and to the modern Kuki-Chin, Naga and Garo <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-289" data-page="289" data-folio="279" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n288_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 279">[p. 279]</button> tribes. Ptolemy places them east of the Ganges, and corroborates the Periplus as to their personal appearance. Lassen (III, 38) identifies the name with the Sanscrit <em>vaishāda</em>, ‘‘wretchedly stupid,’’ and says they were a tribe of Sikkim. Our author locates them ‘‘on the borders of the Land of This,’’ indicating that Tibet was then subject to China. The location of their annual fair must have been near the modern Gangtok (27° 20′ N., 88° 38′ E.) above which the Cho-La or the Jelap-La Pass leads to Chumbi on the Tibetan side of the frontier, from which the overland route mentioned in § 64 led across the table-land to Koko Nor, Siningfu and Singanfu. Other passes through Nepāl are possible, particularly that by the Arun River, but the route through Sikkim involves the least deviation from the direct line from Koko Nor to the Ganges; while from Gyangste to the source of the Arun a pass must be scaled higher by 3000 feet than Jelap-La. (See Freshfield, <em>The Roads to Tibet</em>, in <em>Geographical Journal</em>, xxiii, Jan. and March, 1904; and <em>The Highest Mountain in the World</em>, <em>ibid.</em>, xxi, March, 1903;—O’Connor, <em>Routes in Sikkim</em>;—Louis, <em>Gates of Tibet</em>.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pseudo-Callisthenes (III, 8) refers to the <em>Bisadæ</em> ‘‘who gather a leaf. They are a feeble folk, of very diminutive stature, and live in caves among the rocks. They understand how to climb precipices through their intimate knowledge of the country and are thus able to gather the leaf. They are small men of stunted growth, with big heads of hair which is straight and not cut.’’ (McCrindle, <em>Ancient India</em>, p. 180.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Fergusson (<em>History of Indian Architecture</em>, I, 13) says: ‘‘The Tibetans are a fragment of a great primitive population that occupied both the northern and southern slopes of the Himalayas at some very remote prehistoric time. They were worshippers of trees and serpents; and they, and their descendants and connections, in Bengal, Ceylon, Tibet, Burma, Siam and China, have been the bulwark of Buddhism. In India the Dravidians resisted Buddhism on the south, and a revival of Aryanism abolished it in the north.’’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. <strong>Feast for several days.</strong>—This description of a tribal festival and market resembles many accounts of other primitive peoples. Compare the following from Herodotus (IV, 196):</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘‘The Carthaginians further say that beyond the Pillars of Hercules there is a region of Libya, and men who inhabit it; when they arrive among these people and have unloaded their merchandise, they set it in order on the shore, go on board their ships, and make a great smoke; that the inhabitants, seeing the smoke, come down to the sea, and then deposit gold in exchange for the merchandise, and withdraw <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-290" data-page="290" data-folio="280" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n289_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 280">[p. 280]</button> to some distance from the merchandise; that the Carthaginians then, going ashore, examine the gold, and if the quantity seems sufficient for the merchandise, they take it up and sail away; but if it is not sufficient, they go on board their ships again and wait; the natives <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-291" data-page="291" data-folio="281" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n290_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 281">[p. 281]</button> then approach and deposit more gold, until they have satisfied them; neither party ever wrongs the other; for they do not touch the gold before it is made adequate to the value of the merchandise, nor do the natives touch the merchandise before the other party has taken the gold.”</p></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-032"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-032_p0290.webp" alt="On a modern trade-route through the mountains of Sikkim. The shoulder-baskets and covers of matting are easily distinguishable." loading="lazy"><figcaption>On a modern trade-route through the mountains of Sikkim. The shoulder-baskets and covers of matting are easily distinguishable.</figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Pomponius Mela (III, viii, 60) seems also to speak of the silent trade of the Himalayas; Ammianus Marcellinus, in the passage already quoted, tells of such a custom at Tashkurghan, the “Stone Tower” of the Pamirs, where silk passed from Eastern hands to Western; while Fa-Hien, describing a similar custom in Ceylon, ascribes it to the “spirits and nagas,” the tutelary guardians of the precious articles of trade. (<em>Travels</em>, chap. xxxviii.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. <strong>Great packs and baskets.</strong>—The same thing is in constant use today in this region, being the regular burden of the coolies of Nepāl and Sikkim.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. <strong>Petri.</strong>—Our author is misled by a fancied resemblance to the Greek <em>petros</em>, fiber; the word is the Sanscrit <em>patra</em>, leaf. Otherwise the description of the preparation of the <em>tamala</em> leaves is correct, being corroborated throughout by Pliny.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">65. <strong>Malabathrum.</strong>—The <em>Cinnamomum tamala</em> is native in this part of the Himalayas, being one of the principal trees.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">So Marco Polo (II, xlvi), in his account of <em>Tebet:</em> “It contains in several quarters rivers and lakes, in which gold-dust is found in great abundance. Cinnamon also grows there in great plenty. Coral is in great demand in this country and fetches a high price, for they delight to hang it round the necks of their women and of their idols.” (See pp. 82-4, 87, 89, 216-18, 256.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">66. <strong>Influence of the gods.</strong>—This is still the geography of Brahman writings. Like Tavernier in the 17th century, who summarized the <em>Rāmāyana</em> in his <em>Travels</em>, so this merchant of Berenice in the 1st century came under the spell of the great epics of India, as he sojourned among</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza"><span class="line">“the sister nations three,</span><br>
<span class="line">Cholas, Cheras, and the Pandyas dwelling by the southern sea.”</span></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The region beyond Sikkim, “impassable by reason of its great cold,” and including the mightiest peaks of the Himalayas, was within the sphere of the <em>Kurukshetra</em> of the later <em>Vedas</em>, the <em>Brāhmanas</em>, and the <em>Mahābhārata</em>, the home-land of the Brahman faith; with the greatest of all mountains, Everest, is associated the name of Gaurisankar, a name of Siva and Durgā; in the western curve of the great <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-292" data-page="292" data-folio="282" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n291_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 282">[p. 282]</button> chain is the sacred peak of Kailās, the Olympus of the Hindu gods, the axis of the universe and the way to heaven; while the ending of the Periplus is that of the Sītā-quest in the <em>Rāmāyana</em>:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="verse-stanza"><span class="line">“Halt not till you reach the country where the northern Kurus rest,</span><br>
<span class="line">Utmost confines of the wide earth, home of Gods and Spirits blest!”</span></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-293"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="293" data-folio="283" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/b29353051/page/n292_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 283">[p. 283]</button></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-033"><img src="/figures/periplus-erythraean-sea/fig-033_p0293.webp" alt="Black and white photographic print showing a snow-capped pyramidal mountain peak rising behind dark, snow-streaked rocky mountain ridges and foreground scree slopes." loading="lazy"></figure><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-07-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink">Note.</strong> <span class="fn-body">See Vivien de Saint-Martin, <em>Le Nord de l’Afrique dans l’Antiquité grecque et romaine</em>. Paris, 1863: p. 197.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink">Note.</strong> <span class="fn-body">Samuel Edward Dawson: <em>The Line of Demarcation of Pope Alexander VI, and that of the Treaty of Tordesillas</em>, in Transactions of the Royal Society of Canada, 1899: Vol. V. § 2, pp. 467 ff.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">*.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Cf.</em> Virgil, <em>Georgics</em> II, 116-117: Divisæ arboribus patriæ. Sola India nigrum Fert ebenum, solis est turea virga Sabæis. And again, I, 57: India mittit ebur, molles sua tura Sabæi.</span></li></ol></section>
